Workload Placement

13 Workload Placement

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Physical AI stack focus for this chapter.

Why can a safety check miss its deadline even when it runs on its own processor core?

A correct refusal computed too late does not stop the motion it was meant to prevent. Separating a learned proposer from its permission path in software does not make the two independent while they still share memory, electrical power, clocks, or cooling. A burst of inference can then delay the check that should stop the machine, heat from the accelerator can slow the core that runs that check for seconds after the burst has ended, and a single supply or reset fault can silence both paths at once. None of these couplings appears on a software architecture diagram, and none shows up in average utilization, so a safety argument that has never been loaded on its real silicon rests on an untested premise. Moving the check to separate hardware removes some couplings and adds others, a communication delay and a longer chain of supplies and wiring, so no placement is independent by construction. Where each workload runs therefore decides whether the permission path can still refuse in time, and an action that crosses the causal boundary on a late refusal cannot be taken back.

Learning Objectives
  • Conduct a dependent-failure inventory of the caches, memory controllers, interconnects, supply rails, clocks, resets, and thermal paths that each candidate placement leaves shared between the proposal and permission paths
  • Explain why average bus utilization cannot bound a deadline, and identify which contention mechanism (bandwidth queueing, bank conflicts, or shared-cache miss handling) threatens a declared worst-case execution time
  • Evaluate whether shared power and reset paths can defeat a selected permission placement
  • Analyze thermal coupling and clock throttling hysteresis between multi-tenant workloads across disparate physical timescales
  • Size a windowed watchdog’s service window from justified completion and inter-tick jitter bounds for a non-blocking record exchange
  • Select among static reservation, spatial partitioning, workload reduction, and physical silicon relocation from measured interference and timing allowances, and allocate the dispatch-to-admission interval of an on-board or off-board proposer against the stopping budget
  • Design an adversarial stress campaign whose falsification threshold is the permission path’s declared worst-case execution time (\(\PermissionTickWcet\))
  • Construct a placement record formalizing the deployment envelope that scopes loaded observed maxima, thermal headroom, bus arbitration, mitigations, and untested conditions for release review
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

13.1 The Silicon Substrate

When the warehouse mobile manipulator’s permission check finishes late, its motor drives keep executing the last admitted command, and the loaded base covers distance that the stopping budget of Safety Enforcement never reserved. The control barrier filter, the stopping reservation, and the fallback ladder developed there hold only while the permission path completes its check inside its hard real-time deadline on every cycle. The enforcement record declares a worst-case execution time inside that deadline, a bound on the check running alone, and whether the bound survives while the learned proposers run beside it is a question about the silicon underneath. Workload placement decides which silicon executes each computation, and with that choice, which memories, interconnects, clocks, power supplies, and thermal paths each task shares with the others. In conventional computing, placement is an optimization over throughput, latency, or energy. On a machine whose refusals must arrive on time, it becomes a safety problem.

Modern silicon integration works directly against the separation the permission path needs. A contemporary heterogeneous system-on-chip (SoC) integrates general-purpose application cores, neural accelerators, real-time microcontrollers, and specialized safety islands onto a single monolithic die. On that die, these subsystems share memory controllers, dynamic random-access memory (DRAM) channels, system-level caches, on-chip network interconnects, voltage regulators, clock governors, and the physical package that carries away heat. While silicon integration lowers unit cost, board area, and inter-processor communication latency, it quietly couples through shared hardware the very execution paths that the software architecture keeps apart. The question placement must answer is whether the deterministic permission path can keep its deadline and its authority to refuse unsafe motion while the proposal path runs at full load on shared hardware. Answering it requires a bound on hardware interference for every shared physical resource, derived from worst-case timing analysis and validated under loaded stress tests.

On the warehouse mobile manipulator, the software architecture draws the proposal boundary between the Brain and the Nervous System as a clean logical boundary. Part I adopted a working hypothesis about where that line falls in hardware. That hypothesis places the permission path on a discrete safety microcontroller physically separate from the application SoC. The machine’s two physical jobs show what that separation must guard against. For the mobile base, whose mass-dominated mobility spends stopping clearance, an interconnect stall during visual feature extraction on the proposal engine could delay the permission path’s refusal while the loaded base approaches a rack end. Every tick of delay beyond the single cycle accounted for in the stopping budget is travel distance the machine never reserved. For the arm, whose contact-dominated manipulation spends force margin, a burst of neural accelerator traffic that evicts the permission path’s lookup tables from a shared cache could delay contact force validation while the gripper presses against a spring-latched cage door. The monolithic SoC is therefore an architecture the machine must be able to reject on evidence, and the discrete microcontroller is a design claim that must survive a loaded test rather than be assumed.

↩︎ The independence requirement on the permission path, and the two-processor split as a hypothesis that loaded tests must support, are established in The Independence Requirement and the Two-Processor Hypothesis.

Assigning the permission filter to a dedicated processor core does not create an isolated failure domain. A core running in isolation may still stall on memory arbitration, slow down under dynamic clock throttling, or suffer transient voltage drops when adjacent accelerators become active. Memory protection units and operating system process boundaries enforce spatial partitioning by preventing unauthorized reads and writes across address spaces. However, memory protection hardware governs access permissions rather than access timing. The underlying interconnect crossbar, memory controllers, and DRAM banks determine when memory transactions actually complete. Establishing a sound safety case requires both spatial partitioning to prevent memory corruption and temporal partitioning to guarantee bounded service times for shared hardware resources (Kopetz 2011; Rushby 1999).

Definition 13.1: Operational independence

Operational independence is the property that no activity, fault, or overload in the proposal domain can push the permission path’s execution past its deadline or disable its authority to refuse, through any resource the two paths share.

  1. Significance: It is the premise under which the stopping budget charges exactly one permission tick of detection delay. Every tick that proposal-side load adds beyond that one is travel at speed \(\SpeedAtDetection\) that the irreversibility test \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\) never reserved, and a stall of unbounded length leaves the test with no bound at all.
  2. Distinction: Unlike spatial isolation, which private address spaces and separate cores supply, operational independence also covers time, supply, clock, reset, and heat. It is the placement-level form of what functional-safety standards call freedom from interference, the absence of cascading failures from one element into another of higher integrity (International Organization for Standardization 2018).
  3. Common pitfall: Arguing independence from the separation alone. A separate core, or even a separate chip, is the hypothesis the Nervous System adopted, not its proof; independence is argued one shared resource at a time, and a loaded test on the real silicon can refute the claim for any one of them.

Every placement claim must therefore name the physical and architectural resources that could delay or disable enforcement, alongside the mechanisms that bound their mutual interference. A shared die and a discrete safety microcontroller are competing hypotheses about those hardware dependencies, not interchangeable processor choices. Testing either hypothesis starts with what each path computes and the deadline it must meet.

13.2 Two Paths and the Resources They Share

The third law, proposal is not permission, separates the machine’s two paths in authority; placement decides whether the hardware keeps them separate in time when the proposer is working hardest. Whether a candidate placement preserves that separation depends on the execution profile of each path, the physical resources that could couple them beneath software access controls, and which of those resources remain shared across the compute board.

If a 40 ms neural proposer and a 400 μs-deadline safety loop share the same operating-system scheduler or memory controller, a single kernel preemption tail or memory-bus burst can stall the 1 kHz permission tick past its deadline. To prevent that stall, the mobile manipulator splits control across two execution paths with different systems requirements. The proposal path runs the chunk policy on the application processor at 20 Hz, ingesting synchronized navigation and wrist camera streams to emit coordinated base velocity and arm joint targets. Its 99th-percentile (p99) inference latency, vision processing included, consumes 40 ms (an illustrative benchmark; see the Reader Guide) of each 50 ms chunk period. Running in unprivileged user space, the proposal path holds no direct hardware authority over the motor drives. In the reference design from Part I, the permission path executes on a discrete safety microcontroller (MCU) at 1 kHz. On every tick, it checks the active trajectory and any candidate replacement against the stopping envelope from Stopping Envelopes, staging admitted setpoints into the outbound EtherCAT frame destined for the drives. This routine must finish within a chosen 400 μs deadline inside its 1 ms tick. It runs in a privileged real-time domain on a dual-core lockstep controller,1 whose cycle-by-cycle comparator flags divergent execution. That hardware guard is needed because a software envelope check cannot diagnose a silent fault in the CPU core executing it. Only the permission path holds memory-mapped input-output (MMIO) access to the fieldbus master carrying drive commands.

1 Dual-Core Lockstep: Dual-core lockstep runs identical instruction streams across two physical cores, typically offset by several cycles so that a single transient fault cannot corrupt both pipelines in the same way, while a hardware comparator flags any divergence. While lockstep execution catches internal CPU faults, it cannot catch bad envelopes computed from corrupted sensor inputs or common-mode voltage sags on shared power rails. Lockstep is therefore one piece of the safety microcontroller’s watchdog design rather than a complete integrity defense.

Definition 13.2: Heterogeneous silicon partitioning

Heterogeneous silicon partitioning is the architectural allocation of stochastic, high-capacity cognitive workloads (perception, learned policy evaluation) and deterministic safety loops (safety invariant checks, admission and fallback) across specialized execution domains isolated in hardware.

  1. Significance: Placing high-capacity neural deliberation and real-time safety enforcement on the same general-purpose operating system or compute engine creates common-mode failures. Kernel thread preemption, dynamic memory allocation, and time-slicing between accelerator contexts add tail delay that no measured average bounds, and a permission path exposed to that tail can miss its physical control deadline.
  2. Distinction: Unlike logical software process isolation (such as Linux containers or POSIX process priorities), silicon partitioning enforces isolation across independent hardware registers, tightly coupled memories (TCM), and dedicated peripheral buses.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating multicore CPU affinity as failure-domain separation. Cores on one die still share caches, memory controllers, clock trees, and power rails, the resources that operational independence (definition 13.1) must bound one by one.

These two processors run separate software stacks. Proposal inference, global scene-map updates, telemetry streaming, and fleet networking run on the application processor under Linux. While a real-time kernel patch shortens scheduler dispatch latency, it cannot bound latency tails added by unvetted device drivers, bus arbitration, or shared memory controllers.2 Similarly, zero-copy buffers reduce internal memory-bus traffic, but they do not remove contention at the physical memory controller.

2 PREEMPT_RT: The Linux real-time patch set makes most kernel code preemptible and runs interrupt handlers as schedulable threads, and it was merged into the mainline kernel in Linux 6.12 in 2024 after about two decades out of tree (KernelNewbies 2024). It shortens the scheduler’s latency tail but cannot bound delays that drivers, firmware, or shared memory add, so a PREEMPT_RT host needs a measured bound before it may carry any part of the permission tick.

KernelNewbies. 2024. Linux 6.12. Kernel release summary.

3 MPU and IOMMU: A memory protection unit (MPU) checks each CPU access against a few fixed address regions without translating addresses, so its cost is constant and analyzable. An input-output memory management unit (IOMMU) applies the same check to direct memory access (DMA) from accelerators and camera interfaces. Both govern where a memory transaction may access without arbitrating when the interconnect serves it; they enforce spatial partitioning while delegating deadline guarantees to hardware bus arbiters.

By contrast, enforcement and sensor validation run on the discrete safety microcontroller as bare-metal firmware governed by a hardware memory protection unit (MPU).3 Quadrature encoder counters, the inertial measurement unit (IMU) on its Serial Peripheral Interface (SPI) bus, and hardware timer capture channels are mapped directly to dedicated physical pins. Each candidate proposal arrives from the application processor across a board-level serial link and is published into local memory via the seqlock mailbox of Lock-free and wait-free mailbox exchanges. No dynamic random-access memory (DRAM) is shared between the two paths. The memory protection unit isolates local state, while the dedicated peripheral bus insulates the enforcer’s execution path from the host’s memory traffic.

Admitting an entire plan is a different computation from running the cyclic permission tick. For example, the whole-plan validation routine of Action chunk horizons and reaction lag cannot run uninterrupted inside a 1 ms control loop without breaking timing guarantees. Instead, the runtime scheduler must preserve the real-time tick either by running admission as bounded, interruptible work or by moving it to a separate processor whose own timing is verified. In either case, admission ends with an atomic check of current state and sensor evidence right before activation. The running machine splits admission across these domains. The host performs the global geometry checks of Admission, Cyclic Checking, and Monitor Independence. The microcontroller checks the resulting record against bounded local evidence as background work below the tick’s priority, so the tick preempts it. Section 13.6.2 lists the bounds that keep this background work from delaying the tick through the microcontroller’s own buses and memory. The resource budget must cover the active trajectory buffer, the resident fallback plan, the temporary admission workspace, and worst-case interruption overheads. The proposal generation rate also does not set the interval between successful admissions. Qualification must show that the end-to-end pipeline (transfer, admission, and activation) preserves the lease renewal bound, a guarantee the tick deadline alone cannot provide.

Similarly, the full volumetric scene map stays on the application processor. The permission path receives only a compact, bounded local evidence snapshot whose spatial coverage, state uncertainty, observation timestamp, expiration horizon, and transport integrity have been pre-validated for the proposed motion and its fallback maneuver. Updating the host map, serializing the snapshot, transporting it across the bus, validating admission, storing it locally, and running per-tick spatial queries are separate computational costs that need their own entries in the timing budget. A synchronous query to the host processor must never stall the permission tick. If a proposed trajectory strays outside the validated bounds or lifetime of the local snapshot, the MCU rejects it immediately. The microcontroller’s static memory budget must therefore show that the local snapshot buffer and resident fallback trajectories fit alongside the compiled enforcement binary.

Electrically, the power delivery network isolates the microcontroller, low-voltage drive logic, and electromechanical spring-brake coils onto a dedicated safety rail. This rail carries a local capacitive hold-up of 2 s and draws zero load from the proposal subsystem. Hardware qualification must verify that voltage transients from motor acceleration or neural accelerator bursts on the shared battery pack cannot trigger an undervoltage reset on this isolated rail. This local energy reserve keeps the logic alive and holds the mechanical brakes released throughout a controlled stop without supplying traction power. Achieving the powered deceleration profiles of The four rungs of the fallback ladder requires both an intact high-voltage drive path and a sink for regenerative braking energy. If either fails, the system must trigger a certified fail-safe fault response rather than relying on powered stopping-distance assumptions.

↩︎ The isolated permission rail and its hold-up sizing are established in Cross-budget coupling and permission rail isolation.

On an unloaded machine, the initial compute budgets look generous. Representative baseline costs on the safety microcontroller allocate 40 μs to sample motor encoders, read the IMU over SPI, and check the proposal’s evidence epoch. They allocate 80 μs to check kinematic and dynamic envelope constraints and 15 μs to stage admitted setpoints into the outbound drive frame. That gives an unloaded enforcer execution time of 135 μs, or 54,000 cycles at the microcontroller’s 400 MHz clock. Measured against the 400 μs hard deadline, this baseline path leaves 265 μs of slack. Even the declared worst-case execution time (WCET) of 250 μs, the design envelope from Execution timing in the sub-millisecond loop, leaves 150 μs of headroom.

This timing margin cannot be spent entirely on shared-hardware contention. The response bound of equation 4.3 already charges the tick for blocking \(\TaskBlockingTime_i\) by lower-priority tasks (50 μs) and interference \(\TaskInterferenceTime_i\) from higher-priority tasks and interrupts (40 μs), both from the microcontroller’s own workload (Response time analysis and execution variance). Delay from hardware shared with the proposer is a separate cost because it lengthens the execution term \(\WorstCaseExecutionTime_i\) itself. A loaded tick meets its deadline only if its execution time, including all shared-hardware interference, stays below the deadline minus those internal overheads (90 μs), leaving an execution ceiling of 310 μs. Under the declared WCET bound, shared-hardware contention can add at most 60 μs of delay before the tick misses its deadline, and every placement analysis must account for this full budget.

The declared bound, not the deadline, is what a placement must defend. It bounds the tick’s execution in isolation, and the chosen placement claims that proposal-side load adds nothing to it. A loaded tick that runs longer than 250 μs therefore refutes that claim even when it finishes before the 400 μs deadline, because every margin downstream of the bound was computed on the premise it just broke. Each coupling in this chapter is priced against that falsification threshold, which section 13.7 formalizes and turns into a test campaign.

On the application processor, meanwhile, the neural policy’s 40 ms inference latency leaves 10 ms of margin within its 50 ms period. The enforcer’s sub-costs above are a baseline model rather than an integrated measurement, and local spatial-query overheads must also fit inside the cyclic budget. Full qualification requires measuring the loaded control cycle on the target to establish which spatial queries fall inside constraint evaluation and to account for any separate overheads. Neither an unloaded benchmark nor an isolated query model can verify the declared WCET bound.

↩︎ The spatial-query workload the safety microcontroller must answer each tick is established in Volumetric metric maps and distance fields.

13.2.1 Shared failure domains and attack surfaces

A single stalls-per-tick coupling across shared silicon can consume the entire 60 μs shared-hardware allowance even when the proposal and permission paths run on separate processor cores.4 Beneath software access controls, four physical channels can bind the two paths without appearing on a software architecture diagram: memory subsystems, power rails, thermal paths, and clock distribution networks. The physical architecture from Part I is designed to sever each of them at the board level.

4 Multicore Interference Certification (FAA AC 20-193): Avionics met this problem first. The 2016 CAST-32A position paper named shared caches, interconnects, and memory controllers as interference channels, and FAA Advisory Circular AC 20-193, issued in 2024 and aligned with EASA’s AMC 20-193, replaced it with formal acceptance criteria (Federal Aviation Administration 2024). Its lesson for this machine is that a timing bound measured on one core is void on a multicore until every shared row of table 1 has been analyzed or mitigated.

Federal Aviation Administration. 2024. Use of Multi-Core Processors. AC 20-193. U.S. Department of Transportation, Federal Aviation Administration.

5 TLB Shootdown: When an operating system modifies page tables, it broadcasts inter-processor interrupts (IPIs) to invalidate stale Translation Lookaside Buffer entries across all active cores. Receiving cores must pause thread execution and flush affected entries, adding unbudgeted multi-microsecond stalls. On shared silicon, these synchronization pauses break the cycle determinism needed by inner motor loops.

6 DMA Cache Coherence: Direct memory access transfers bypass CPU caches, requiring explicit cache-line invalidation or writeback to keep peripherals and cores consistent. Without hardware snoop filtering or explicit barriers, processors read stale sensor frames or overwrite pending actuator commands. These cache maintenance steps inject variable delay into high-rate control loops unless static, uncached SRAM regions are dedicated to peripheral transfers.

Memory contention is the first and often most severe coupling. On cores sharing an address space, operating-system page-table updates trigger inter-processor interrupts for Translation Lookaside Buffer (TLB) shootdowns,5 while coherent direct memory access (DMA) from high-throughput camera pipelines incurs cache-invalidation stalls.6 More fundamentally, a time-critical control loop cannot read its sensor buffers if an asynchronous accelerator monopolizes the physical interconnect. In a Unified Memory Architecture (UMA), neural accelerators, application cores, and real-time cores arbitrate for a single pool of DRAM across a shared crossbar. A high-resolution vision pipeline or an aggressive DMA engine can then saturate the bus and starve the permission routine of encoder and IMU feedback, whatever its software thread priority. This mismatch between software priority and unmanaged interconnect contention is the UMA crossbar trap, and section 13.3 prices the queueing delays and bank conflicts behind it. The chosen design avoids the trap by giving the safety controller exclusive ownership of its memory. Candidate proposals cross an isolated board-level serial bus into dedicated local SRAM, so no DRAM is shared between the two domains.

Power delivery represents the second coupling vector. Although the host processor and the safety controller draw from a common battery, they do not share a power rail. The host application processor draws from the nominal 47.7 V motor drive bus through a dedicated DC-DC step-down converter that triggers an undervoltage lockout (UVLO) if the supply sags to 33 V. The steady-state margin analysis in Electrical Power Integrity ensures that even a depleted battery pack remains above this threshold during simultaneous drive acceleration and peak neural inference. Unmodeled transients or supply faults (a blown fuse, or a battery management system (BMS) contactor trip), however, can depress the bus and crash the application processor. Had the safety microcontroller shared this unisolated supply, that same brownout would reset the safety monitor simultaneously. Motor gate drivers incorporate internal hardware protections for such events: integrated undervoltage lockouts disable power MOSFET outputs when gate-drive rails collapse, and pull-down resistors prevent floating logic pins from triggering destructive shoot-through currents. Those protections mitigate electrical switching faults; they do not establish a harmless coast or a controlled stop. A shared brownout would also extinguish the permission path, leaving no MCU-controlled response to manage the robot’s kinetic energy. The dedicated, held-up rail preserves supervision while the host browns out and reboots. The safety microcontroller detects the bus undervoltage interrupt and commands the fault response whose resources survive. Powered deceleration requires an intact drive-energy path and regenerative sink; if the supply fault removes either, the separately qualified mechanical or passive response governs.

Thermal dissipation and clock distribution introduce the third and fourth coupling channels. When co-located on a shared silicon die or bonded to a common heat spreader, heavy sustained compute workloads on the application processor raise junction temperatures toward thermal trip points. Dynamic voltage and frequency scaling (DVFS) governors configured to protect the silicon package can throttle clock frequencies in shared clock domains, slowing real-time safety cores that share no code with the proposal pipeline. As demonstrated in section 13.4, thermal throttling introduces hysteresis that outlasts the transient compute burst that triggered it. Similarly, relying on a shared external crystal oscillator creates a single point of failure, because thermal drift, mechanical shock, or a fractured crystal desynchronizes both execution domains at once. A discrete microcontroller removes the shared die and oscillator dependencies by providing a physically separated package, dedicated heat dissipation, and an independent quartz crystal oscillator. Its own clock, supply, and thermal operating limits, including exposure to the common chassis environment, still require qualification.

Any safety-critical design must still evaluate the alternative it rejects, a single shared die carrying both paths. On one die the four couplings collapse into one semiconductor package, so real-time cores that execute their instructions independently of the application cores remain coupled through each resource. Bursts of camera DMA, neural accelerator memory access, and high-volume diagnostic logging can stall the real-time cores’ cache fills and delay their interrupt handling unless hardware arbiters enforce deterministic bandwidth caps. At the same time, high-slew current transients can droop a shared core rail and a software-initiated host reset can take down a common reset chain unless power and supervisor domains are strictly isolated. Even when memory and power are partitioned, heat from the neural processing units raises the substrate temperature under the real-time cores, which share the package’s only route to the chassis. A unified on-die governor throttles the real-time cores’ clock together with the accelerator’s when that substrate temperature reaches its trip point. A shared PLL or crystal also carries any clock fault to both.

Beyond accidental contention, these shared silicon pathways expose a cyber-physical attack vector. An adversary who compromises the unprivileged proposal stack can generate malicious memory traffic or toggle accelerator power states rapidly. The traffic starves the permission routine of memory service, and coordinated current surges pull the shared supply down (section 13.3 prices both). An isolation claim must therefore hold against the worst contention the proposal domain can generate within its authorized hardware limits, and benchmarks of benign workloads cannot establish that bound.

Functional-safety engineering provides formal tools to analyze this shared inventory. On the mobile manipulator, the intended certified safety scope is deliberately narrow; this illustrative platform has not established certification. The trajectory admission algorithms that validate learned proposals carry no formal safety integrity rating; the intended integrity claims belong to the scoped protective stop functions implemented in the motor drives and safety laser scanner. The function selected for a safety rating on the microcontroller is the watchdog that revokes authority when the chunk lease expires or the host heartbeat falls silent. It then commands a certified drive-level stop, safe stop 1 (SS1, a controlled deceleration followed by torque removal) or safe torque off (STO), whichever the risk assessment assigns to the machine’s state. Keeping that intended rated function apart from unrated software components (the trajectory admission checks and the neural proposer) is a formal safety decomposition. The decomposition requires evidence that faults and interference from the unrated elements cannot defeat the claimed protective functions under the declared fault model. Workload placement determines which shared dependencies that evidence must address. A dependent failure analysis identifies the shared hardware resources whose failure or overload could compromise both paths at once. ISO 26262 Part 9 (International Organization for Standardization 2018) requires it, and IEC 61508 (International Electrotechnical Commission 2010) treats it as common-cause failure analysis. The warehouse machine answers to industrial and mobile-robot safety standards rather than automotive ones (ISO 10218-2:2025, for instance, incorporates the collaborative-robot protections first defined in ISO/TS 15066 (International Organization for Standardization 2025, 2016)), but the requirement of freedom from interference is the same in every domain. The resource audit of this chapter is that dependent-failure analysis carried out on physical silicon, with every coupling priced in the tick’s timing budget.

International Electrotechnical Commission. 2010. IEC 61508:2010: Functional Safety of Electrical/Electronic/Programmable Electronic Safety-Related Systems. International Electrotechnical Commission.
International Organization for Standardization. 2025. ISO 10218-2:2025: Robotics, Safety Requirements, Part 2: Industrial Robot Applications and Robot Cells. International Organization for Standardization.
International Organization for Standardization. 2016. ISO/TS 15066:2016: Robots and Robotic Devices, Collaborative Robots. International Organization for Standardization.

↩︎ The certified drive functions beneath the permission path, and the stop each failure triggers, are established in Certified safety functions beneath the permission path.

↪ The industrial and mobile-robot standards that govern this machine, and where each one’s evidence is generated, are developed in The standards map for the running machine.

Because each shared dependency names a physical or adversarial mechanism that could defeat the decomposition, qualification must test the isolation by injecting faults rather than by reviewing schematics. The timing stress campaign designed in section 13.7 validates these boundaries against the declared WCET threshold across four stress regimes, three aimed at the supply, clock, and link and one at the proposals themselves:

  1. Power-rail droop testing: Injecting maximum-slew current transients into the drive bus to verify that the noise coupled onto the safety rail stays inside the microcontroller’s specified supply tolerance, the limit the placement record’s droop field carries.
  2. Clock glitching and frequency margining: Verifying that the real-time core maintains deterministic instruction cycle counts under clock jitter and voltage fluctuation.
  3. Communication link flooding: Saturating shared interconnects to confirm that safety-critical heartbeat frames and trajectory proposals retain their deterministic transmission windows.7
  4. Numerical and semantic fault injection: Streaming synthetic proposals populated with out-of-range floats, subnormal numbers, and IEEE 754 NaNs to verify single-cycle rejection without pipeline stalls or exception traps.

7 CAN Arbitration: Controller Area Network protocols resolve bus contention at the physical layer using wired-AND dominant (‘0’) and recessive (‘1’) signaling. If a defective transceiver continuously drives a dominant state (a “babbling idiot” fault), it monopolizes the shared wire and blocks all other traffic. Hardware bus guardians and dominant-timeout clamp circuits time each transmission and physically disconnect jabbering nodes to keep the bus available for remaining safety nodes.

↪ The physical injection apparatus for supply, clock, and bus disturbances is developed in Fault Injection on Hardware.

13.2.2 Four candidate placements against one shared-resource inventory

On the mobile manipulator’s compute board, four candidate placements for the permission loop span the options from sharing every silicon resource to sharing only the battery (table 1). Scoring each candidate row by row exposes the common-cause faults that a high-level chip block diagram hides, including whether a commercial module actually routes an on-die safety island’s pins and rails to the integrator. At the least isolated extreme, shared application cores (a) expose the permission loop to operating-system scheduling jitter, driver interrupt stalls, and kernel preemption tails, delays that can push execution past the 400 μs deadline and even the 1 ms control period. Moving to a real-time cluster in the host clock and power domain (b) dedicates execution cores to safety enforcement, yet it still shares the die’s cache hierarchy, memory controller, core supply rail, clock governor, and thermal path. Section 13.3 prices its memory exposure, and section 13.4 shows the clock throttling that rules this candidate out.

To sever those host-domain couplings without leaving the package, modern automotive and industrial SoCs provide an on-die safety island (c). For instance, the Functional Safety Island of NVIDIA’s Orin-class SoCs integrates four dual-core lockstep Cortex-R52 processors, each with private tightly coupled memory, around 3 MB of on-island SRAM (NVIDIA 2023b). The island runs from an independent crystal oscillator and a dedicated voltage rail behind hardware firewalls that block unauthorized access from the rest of the die. The application governor therefore cannot throttle its clock, and an enforcement routine whose code and data stay in island memory never waits on host DRAM arbitration. Even so, several couplings remain inside the package: shared substrate temperature, common reset and power sequencing logic, arbitration across the on-chip fabric carrying proposals or island DMA, and the common board-level power supply. Adopting an on-die island therefore requires reading back the configured firewall registers, tracing the power-sequencing logic, and running the loaded stress campaign of section 13.7 while the safety loop executes. Where those remaining die-level couplings cannot be bounded or the module does not expose the island, a discrete safety microcontroller (d) removes every on-die coupling altogether, trading silicon integration for an explicit board-level link from the application processor that section 13.6 budgets and characterizes.

Laid out on the machine’s compute board, the four candidates differ in how many shared blocks stand between the permission tick and the proposer (figure 1). Candidates (a) and (b) sit behind the application die’s last-level cache, DRAM controller, clock governor, core rail, and fabric. The island (c) moves cores, crystal, and SRAM behind a firewall but stays in the same package and heat path. Only (d), a separate package clocked at 400 MHz with local TCM and a 2 s rail hold-up, leaves the die entirely and reaches it through a checked board link.

On the inventory alone, candidate (c) is a strong contender. It separates the cache and miss handling, DRAM, core rail, and governed clock, the rows behind the strongest mechanisms the next two sections price. It leaves shared the die temperature, the fabric its proposals cross, the board supply, and a reset chain whose independence still needs evidence, each of which a qualification would have to cover. This verdict is provisional, since nothing has yet been priced, but the machine does not wait on it, because it adopts candidate (d) for a reason outside silicon capability. While the safety island exists on the physical SoC die, commercial edge modules packaging the application processor do not expose it to system integrators, reserving access for dedicated industrial platforms (table 2). Because an unexposed, unprogrammable safety island cannot serve as a valid deployment target, safety enforcement must move off the primary module.

Commercial platform exposure is therefore itself an architectural constraint. A vendor block diagram illustrating isolated silicon domains establishes what the hardware could theoretically execute, but system developers can use only the execution resources unlocked by board support packages and platform firmware. While specific commercial product tiers change over time, the engineering requirement to audit software accessibility before committing to a workload placement remains permanent.

The third law (principle 3) says a proposal is not permission, and that separation is empty if the proposal can delay the permission decision through hardware the two share. The four candidates differ in which resources they force the paths to share, and even for the discrete microcontroller the separation remains a hypothesis about timing under load that only a measurement of the loaded tick can support.

Choosing candidate (d) before pricing the shared die does not make the quantitative analysis of shared resources superfluous, for three reasons. First, each mechanism priced in section 13.3 and section 13.4 turns the rejection of candidates (a) and (b) from an architectural assumption into empirical evidence. Second, each mechanism names an obligation that the discrete design carries in miniature, because the microcontroller has its own DMA engines, interrupt controller, internal buses, and power supply, as detailed in section 13.6.2 and section 13.7. Finally, the same analysis provides the qualification blueprint for an integrator whose platform firmware exposes an on-die safety island. Unless a passage says otherwise, the calculations in section 13.3 and section 13.4 evaluate the permission tick placed hypothetically on the application die, using the sub-costs and timing budget assigned to the microcontroller. On a shared die, the first place proposal-side load encounters that tick is the memory system.

13.3 Contention for Shared Resources

Had the mobile manipulator placed its permission path on the application SoC, camera DMA, neural weight fetches, telemetry writes, cache writebacks, and enforcement reads would compete for memory service. Their simultaneous bursts determine the queue seen by the enforcer. On the selected machine, those host-side bursts delay the observation and chunk rather than the permission tick. The permission path encounters their effects as evidence age, refusing stale evidence and entering fallback if no admitted chunk renews the lease.

↩︎ How host-side memory contention, including camera DMA, ages observations is established in Ingestion Overhead and Memory Bus Contention.

13.3.1 DRAM bank conflicts, AXI4 burst slicing, and arbiter starvation

A small urgent read can wait behind a large transfer even when the memory bus is mostly idle over a longer interval. The deadline depends on the traffic already ahead of that read and on whether the controller lets it pass. This queueing delay, which arises when memory and DMA transactions share an interconnect and controller, is the mechanism inside the UMA crossbar trap, and its bound depends on transaction size, queue policy, and bank timing.

Each claimant’s average demand follows from its transfer size and event rate. On a shared multi-channel LPDDR5 interface, camera DMA streams, weight fetches at the policy rate, the enforcer’s reads, drive writes, cache writebacks, and logging together occupy a modest fraction of theoretical peak bandwidth on average. An engineer who inspects only that average concludes that most of the memory bus remains free. Memory crossbar arbiters and DRAM schedulers do not schedule average rates. They arbitrate discrete, bursty transactions, and two workloads with identical average throughput interfere with radically different latency profiles if one streams in small, sporadic packets while the other arrives in long, saturating blocks. The weight transfers that set the memory wall (Wulf and McKee 1995) of The memory wall are the long blocks here, and on a shared controller the proposer’s throughput limit becomes the permission path’s queue.

8 AXI4 (Advanced eXtensible Interface 4): Arm’s AMBA on-chip bus protocol permits incrementing bursts of up to 256 beats and forbids any burst from crossing a 4,096-byte address boundary. At a 16-byte beat, one full burst is exactly one such page, which is why the notebook below splits a weight tile into one transaction per page. An urgent read that waits for the granted burst to finish, as this example assumes, is a property of the chosen arbiter, not an AXI guarantee.

How long a safety read waits depends on transaction arbitration and DRAM scheduling. An accelerator moving weights in large 256 KB tiles generates discrete memory transactions across the internal system interconnect. The example assumes an arbiter that completes each granted burst before serving a different requester.8 Although bus protocols provide quality-of-service (QoS) priority signals (ARQOS), commodity memory controllers frequently treat these tags as advisory unless strict hardware priority bypass with bounded preemption latency is explicitly certified in register configurations. Whether an in-flight transfer can delay the urgent read, and whether queued tensor transactions can pass it, depends on the selected interconnect and arbiter. AXI burst limits alone do not establish a service-time or starvation bound.

Every memory request passes through four stages: the requester issues it, the interconnect arbiter grants it a slot, the memory controller queues it, and the target bank serves it. Each DRAM bank holds one open row in its row buffer. An LPDDR5 channel has 16 banks, divided into 4 bank groups at the higher data rates, or 8 larger banks, each formed by merging two, in an alternative mode (Steiner et al. 2022). A read to the open row (a row hit) needs only a column access. A read to a different row of the same bank (a row conflict) must first close the open row with a precharge (\(\RowPrechargeTime\)) and then open the new one with an activation (\(\RowActivationDelay\)). The memory standard sets a minimum time in nanoseconds for each step, 18 ns for both, and the controller can only wait whole cycles of its command clock. At the 6,400 MT/s data rate of the machine’s application SoC, that clock ticks every 1.25 ns, each step rounds up to 15 cycles, or 18.75 ns, and a conflict adds 37.5 ns (Luo et al. 2024). Bank-group spacing, refresh, and bus turnaround add further terms, which DRAM Contention and Interconnect Arbitration treats.

Steiner, Lukas, Matthias Jung, Michael Huonker, and Norbert Wehn. 2022. “Unveiling the Real Performance of LPDDR5 Memories.” Proceedings of the International Symposium on Memory Systems (MEMSYS), 1–3. https://doi.org/10.1145/3565053.3565062.
Luo, Haocong, Yahya Can Tuğrul, F. Nisa Bostancı, Ataberk Olgun, A. Giray Yağlıkçı, and Onur Mutlu. 2024. “Ramulator 2.0: A Modern, Modular, and Extensible DRAM Simulator.” IEEE Computer Architecture Letters 23 (1): 112–16. https://doi.org/10.1109/LCA.2023.3333759.
Rixner, Scott, William J. Dally, Ujval J. Kapasi, Peter Mattson, and John D. Owens. 2000. “Memory Access Scheduling.” International Symposium on Computer Architecture (ISCA), 128–38. https://doi.org/10.1145/339647.339668.

The controller also decides the order of service. Controllers that use first-ready, first-come-first-served (FR-FCFS) scheduling serve ready row hits before older requests that need a new row, which maximizes throughput (Rixner et al. 2000). A streaming accelerator supplies an unbroken run of row hits across its weight matrices, so an FR-FCFS scheduler can keep passing the safety core’s sporadic read until an internal starvation counter expires. Under a FIFO queue that lets no later arrival pass the safety read, the idealized wait is \[\MemoryBusQueueDelay = \sum_{k \in \QueuedTransactionSet} \frac{{\QueuedTransactionBytes}_{k}}{\text{BW}_{\text{peak}}} + \RowConflictCount (\RowPrechargeTime + \RowActivationDelay) \tag{1}\] where \(\QueuedTransactionSet\) holds the transactions ahead of the read, \({\QueuedTransactionBytes}_{k}\) is each one’s byte count, \(\RowConflictCount\) counts changes to a different row within an already active bank, and \(\text{BW}_{\text{peak}}\) is an optimistic service rate. The application SoC achieves that aggregate peak bandwidth across parallel 16-bit LPDDR5 channels, each containing its own sixteen internal banks, so dividing a backlog by \(\text{BW}_{\text{peak}}\) holds only when physical address bits interleave transactions evenly across all channels rather than funneling them into a single channel queue. Arbitration overhead and refresh add to the result. Exceeding the sensing allowance first violates the allocation to that subtask; it does not by itself imply that the whole tick misses its deadline. The worked comparison below keeps the sense allocation, declared execution bound, and physical deadline separate.

Napkin Math 13.1: The backlog budget
Problem: Suppose the same permission tick, with the same sub-costs and the same blocking and interference budget, ran on a core of the application SoC and read its sensor snapshot from the SoC’s shared DRAM. How much queued traffic could precede that read before the tick exceeds its sense allowance, its declared worst-case execution time, and its deadline?

Variables:

  • Sense allowance 40 μs and 95 μs of constraint evaluation and drive-frame write (Illustrative sub-costs), declared worst-case execution time \(\PermissionTickWcet =\) 250 μs and deadline \(\EnforcerDeadline =\) 400 μs (Chosen), with 90 μs of blocking and interference charged as in equation 4.3 (Illustrative).
  • Peak DRAM bandwidth \(\text{BW}_{\text{peak}} =\) 204 GB/s (Datasheet), served at that aggregate rate because the controller interleaves addresses across all channels (Illustrative premise).
  • One backlog ahead of the read: a weight tile (64 transactions), a camera transfer, and a telemetry record, 88 transactions and 360,448 bytes, with 3 same-bank row conflicts (Illustrative), each costing \(\RowPrechargeTime + \RowActivationDelay =\) 37.5 ns (Datasheet). The snapshot itself is 4,096 bytes (Illustrative).

Math: By equation 1, one backlog waits 360,448 B \(/\) 204 GB/s \(+\) 3 \(\times\) 37.5 ns \(=\) 1.879 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\), and the snapshot then reads in 0.020 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\). The number of backlogs \(\AdmissibleBacklogCount\) that fit a read ceiling \(\ReadTimeCeiling\) is \(\lfloor (\ReadTimeCeiling - \SnapshotReadTime)/\MemoryBusQueueDelay \rfloor\). The sense allowance gives \(\lfloor (\) 40 \(-\) 0.020 \()/\) 1.879 \(\rfloor =\) 21. The declared bound leaves 155 μs for the read after the 95 μs of later work, so it is crossed beyond 82 backlogs. The deadline, less blocking and interference, leaves 310 μs for the tick and 215 μs for the read, and it is crossed beyond 114. With validated priority bypass, only the one in-flight transaction waits ahead, and the read takes 0.04 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\).

Result: Between 82 and 114 backlogs, the tick still meets its deadline but exceeds \(\PermissionTickWcet\). Within this stipulated FIFO model, the fixed isolated sub-costs remain unchanged, so the excess comes from shared-memory queueing. It refutes the placement’s timing premise even though this tick was on time. A measured overrun would instead require distinguishing an underestimated baseline from interference, as section 13.7 explains.

Systems insight: The read’s bound belongs to the memory controller, not to the workload. The bound exists only if the controller limits how much traffic it admits ahead of a safety read or lets that read bypass the queue. An FR-FCFS controller serving a streaming accelerator provides neither unless its configuration is qualified to do so. At the SoC’s full bandwidth, though, plain queueing is a weak threat, since the allowance absorbs 21 backlogs. The interleaving premise carries much of that result. If the backlog and the snapshot shared one of the interface’s 16 channels, each backlog would take 28.4 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\), the allowance would absorb 1, and more than 5 would cross the declared bound. The address mapping therefore belongs in the placement evidence. The mechanisms that actually threaten the declared bound are the ones this FIFO model omits, which section 13.3.2 prices next.

Drawn to scale on one 1 ms period, the thresholds show how far the declared bound sits from the deadline and the period (figure 2). The unloaded tick ends at 135 μs. The next three rows carry the largest backlog counts that keep each bound: 21 for the 40 μs sense allowance, 82 for the declared 250 μs, and 114 for the 400 μs deadline once 90 μs is reserved for blocking and interference. The bottom row confines the traffic to one memory channel, where 5 backlogs already reach the declared bound.

13.3.2 Which interference mechanism threatens the declared bound

The notebook’s arithmetic makes the shared die look safe, and that impression is the danger. The permission tick can absorb a stretch of its unloaded 135 μs cycle by a factor of 1.85 before it crosses the declared worst-case execution time. The allowed stretch is 2.30 before the tick crosses the deadline, once blocking and interference take their 90 μs. Bandwidth queueing at the SoC’s full rate does not come close. Two other mechanisms do, and both live in the shared cache rather than in DRAM.

A non-blocking cache tracks each outstanding miss in a miss status holding register (MSHR) and parks each dirty line it evicts in a writeback buffer until DRAM accepts it. Both structures are small and shared by every core behind the cache. When a streaming co-runner keeps them full, each miss of the victim core waits until an entry drains, so the victim’s delay grows with its own miss count times the aggressor’s drain time, not with the bytes it reads. Partitioning cache capacity does not help, because the partition divides the lines and leaves the MSHRs and the writeback buffer shared. Measured on commercial embedded multicores, MSHR contention raised a task’s worst-case execution time by up to 21× with the cache partitioned (Valsan et al. 2016). A deliberate attack on the writeback buffer slowed a victim on a dedicated core by more than 300×, partitioned or not (Bechtel and Yun 2019). DRAM adds its own multiplier on top. An analytical bound for commercial controllers shows that each of the victim’s requests can wait behind every request that other cores may place ahead of it in the same bank. That number is set by queue depth and the reordering window rather than by average bandwidth (Kim et al. 2014).

Kim, Hyoseung, Dionisio de Niz, Björn Andersson, Mark Klein, Onur Mutlu, and Ragunathan Rajkumar. 2014. “Bounding Memory Interference Delay in COTS-Based Multi-Core Systems.” IEEE Real-Time and Embedded Technology and Applications Symposium (RTAS), 145–54. https://doi.org/10.1109/RTAS.2014.6925998.

These slowdowns were measured on other platforms, on victims whose time was mostly memory stall, so they cannot be charged to the enforcer directly. Using them as assumed multipliers on its memory-stall share provides a sensitivity calculation, not a prediction of target behavior. If a fraction \(\CacheMissStallFraction\) of the unloaded tick waits on shared-cache misses and interference slows that fraction by a factor \(\InterferenceSlowdownFactor\) while the rest runs unchanged, the tick takes \(\UnloadedTickExecutionTime[(1-\CacheMissStallFraction) + \CacheMissStallFraction \InterferenceSlowdownFactor] = \UnloadedTickExecutionTime[1 + \CacheMissStallFraction(\InterferenceSlowdownFactor-1)]\). Setting that no larger than \(\PermissionTickWcet\) and solving for \(\CacheMissStallFraction\) shows that the loaded tick stays within the declared bound only while \[\CacheMissStallFraction \le \frac{\PermissionTickWcet/\UnloadedTickExecutionTime - 1}{\InterferenceSlowdownFactor - 1}. \tag{2}\] At the MSHR-class 21×, the declared bound falls once more than 4.3 percent of the tick is miss stall, and the deadline once more than 6.5 percent is. At the writeback-buffer attack’s 300×, the threshold is 0.28 percent. A target profile must establish whether the constraint evaluation exceeds those fractions; touching tables in shared memory alone does not establish its stall share. The mechanism that threatens the declared bound is therefore miss handling in the shared cache, amplified by bank queueing. The only placements that remove it are those whose tick runs from private memory, candidates (c) and (d) of table 1. On (a) and (b), the measured stall share on the target and a regulation mechanism that bounds \(\InterferenceSlowdownFactor\) (section 13.6) are what a qualification must supply.

At a 21× slowdown, 4.3 percent miss stall breaks the bound.

13.3.3 Power-rail interference

When a neural accelerator steps its current draw in nanoseconds on a core supply rail shared with a permission core, the voltage droops across the package inductance and resistance before board regulators can respond. The droop stretches logic gate delays or triggers adaptive clock stretching inside the permission tick. The rail at issue here is not the drive bus or the microcontroller’s isolated rail; it is the on-die core supply that a shared-die placement, candidate (a) or (b), would share with the accelerator inside one package. A sudden accelerator current step lowers the voltage available to every core on that supply, and the first-order package model is \[\CoreRailVoltageDroop = \PackageInductance \frac{d\ConductorCurrent}{d\TimeInstant} + \FiniteChange \ConductorCurrent \cdot \PackageResistance \tag{3}\] The placement decision depends on whether regulation, decoupling, and stored energy keep the permission path inside its characterized operating range. The package hierarchy and transistor-level delay model are developed in Inductive Voltage Droop and Gate Delay Stretch; here the model identifies the disturbance a shared core rail would carry into the permission tick. The chosen placement keeps its core supply out of that package, and its isolated rail must instead survive board-level events (drive-bus sag, a contactor trip, converter dropout), the disturbances of stress regime 1 and the transient generators of section 13.7.

For the illustrative package parameters, a 6 A step with a 2 ns rise through 30 pH gives 90 mV of inductive droop. The same step through 8 m\(\OhmUnit\) gives 48 mV of resistive droop, 138 mV in all from a 0.850 V nominal rail. This pure \(\PackageInductance\,d\ConductorCurrent/d\TimeInstant\) term ignores on-die decoupling capacitance, and does not resolve the actual transient. Power-integrity engineers instead design the delivery network to a target impedance9 across frequency, and the first droop is set by package inductance resonating with on-die capacitance. The alpha-power model10 (Sakurai and Newton 1990) relates the resulting voltage to gate delay: \[\frac{\GatePropagationDelay(\DroopedSupplyVoltage)}{\GatePropagationDelay(\NominalSupplyVoltage)} = \frac{\DroopedSupplyVoltage}{\NominalSupplyVoltage} \left( \frac{\NominalSupplyVoltage - \TransistorThresholdVoltage}{\DroopedSupplyVoltage - \TransistorThresholdVoltage} \right)^\VelocitySaturationExponent\] Here \(\TransistorThresholdVoltage\) is the modeled threshold voltage, stipulated as 0.420 V, and \(\VelocitySaturationExponent\) is the technology-dependent exponent, stipulated as 1.3. With those inputs, the modeled delay increases by 39 percent. This is a sensitivity calculation, not a measured reset threshold or a universal delay increase for every gate, and ignoring decoupling does not establish a guaranteed upper bound on a resonant transient. The stretch lasts nanoseconds, so it does not lengthen the tick by that percentage. It threatens the setup-time margin of the paths switching during the droop, and it grows without bound as the drooped voltage approaches \(\TransistorThresholdVoltage\). Many current processors detect the droop and stretch the clock until the rail recovers (Grenat et al. 2014), which turns a timing violation into lost cycles that the tick must absorb. Device characterization must establish whether the actual critical paths still meet setup time, how many cycles adaptive clocking removes, and whether undervoltage protection trips.

9 Target Impedance: Power-integrity engineers hold the delivery network’s impedance, seen from the die, below the allowed ripple divided by the largest current step, at every frequency the load can excite (Smith et al. 1999). A flat impedance bounds the droop from a current step of any shape, which a single \(\PackageInductance\,d\ConductorCurrent/d\TimeInstant\) term cannot. That documented bound is what a shared-die placement would owe the permission core’s rail, and what the discrete placement owes only its own isolated rail.

Smith, L. D., R. E. Anderson, D. W. Forehand, T. J. Pelc, and T. Roy. 1999. “Power Distribution System Design Methodology and Capacitor Selection for Modern CMOS Technology.” IEEE Transactions on Advanced Packaging 22 (3): 284–91. https://doi.org/10.1109/6040.784476.

10 Alpha-Power Law: The model was proposed in 1990 (Sakurai and Newton 1990) because short-channel transistors no longer followed the long-channel square law: carrier velocity saturates, and drain current grows as \((\SupplyVoltage-\TransistorThresholdVoltage)^\VelocitySaturationExponent\) with \(\VelocitySaturationExponent\) falling from 2 toward 1. Delay therefore depends on the headroom between supply and threshold, not on the supply alone. A low-voltage core chosen for efficiency has the least headroom, so it is also the most droop-sensitive home for the permission path.

Sakurai, Takayasu, and A. Richard Newton. 1990. “Alpha-Power Law MOSFET Model and Its Applications to CMOS Inverter Delay and Other Formulas.” IEEE Journal of Solid-State Circuits 25 (2): 584–94. https://doi.org/10.1109/4.52187.
Grenat, Aaron, Sanjay Pant, Ravinder Rachala, and Samuel Naffziger. 2014. “Adaptive Clocking System for Improved Power Efficiency in a 28nm X86-64 Microprocessor.” IEEE International Solid-State Circuits Conference (ISSCC), 106–7. https://doi.org/10.1109/ISSCC.2014.6757358.
Checkpoint 13.1: Which shared resource breaks the bound

Queueing, miss handling, and rail droop all delay the permission tick through hardware the two paths share. Before moving to heat, confirm that the distinctions among these physical mechanisms are clear:

A separate permission rail removes the modeled shared core-supply path, but the common battery, wiring, converter behavior, and return paths remain qualification obligations. Rail probes and independent timing instruments must observe them under simultaneous motor and proposal load. Droop and queueing both end within microseconds of the burst that causes them. The heat that the same accelerator dissipates does not, and it couples the paths on a timescale a million times longer.

13.4 Thermal and Timing Coupling

A permission core can suffer execution delays long after a heavy proposal workload has terminated, because thermal capacitance traps heat inside the package. While electrical droop and interconnect contention manifest over nanoseconds and microseconds, thermal coupling operates over seconds. When an accelerator and a real-time permission core share a silicon die, a cooling path, and an on-chip power management governor, sustained neural network inference can lower the clock frequency available to safety enforcement, and keep it lowered after the inference stops.

Thermal clock throttling is the automatic, hardware-enforced reduction of processor clock frequency triggered when sustained power dissipation pushes the silicon junction temperature (\(\JunctionTemperature\)) beyond a critical trip threshold (\(\ThrottleTripTemperature\)). On-die thermal sensing diodes alert the power management controller, which scales down clock frequency through hardware dividers or phase-locked loop (PLL) reconfiguration to prevent gate dielectric breakdown and thermal runaway. Because the physical die, package substrate, and heat spreader require time to dissipate this accumulated thermal energy, the reduced clock frequency persists long after the compute burst ends. For a co-located real-time task, this persistent slowdown can turn a nominal execution margin into a deadline overrun that lasts as long as the package takes to cool.

In this illustrative warehouse scenario, the robot’s electronics bay operates at an ambient temperature of 45\(\,^{\circ}\text{C}\). The thermal dissipation path follows the package row of figure 1, shared by every computational component on the application SoC die. The selected warehouse enclosure seals out airborne dust and conductive debris and uses conduction cooling rather than open fan vents. For a shared-die architecture in this enclosure, the conduction path must transfer all heat generated by sustained proposal inference to the chassis without driving the real-time permission core into a throttled clock state.

For a conduction-cooled enclosure, the total thermal resistance from junction to ambient (\(\JunctionAmbientThermalResistance\)) sums three distinct mechanical layers: \[\JunctionAmbientThermalResistance = \JunctionCaseThermalResistance + \InterfaceMaterialThermalResistance + \ChassisThermalResistance.\] Here, \(\JunctionCaseThermalResistance\) is the junction-to-case resistance of the silicon package, \(\InterfaceMaterialThermalResistance\) is the resistance through the thermal interface material, and \(\ChassisThermalResistance\) represents heat conduction through the metal enclosure to the ambient air. Over extended operation, mechanical shock and thermal cycling can degrade the interface material, increasing \(\InterfaceMaterialThermalResistance\). Simultaneously, battery discharge losses and motor drive electronics inject parasitic heat into the bay; battery Joule heating scales with the square of the discharge current (\(\ConductorCurrent^2 \ElectricalResistance\)). A robust thermal envelope must therefore account for both the mechanical cooling path and the peak concurrent actuator workload. A quiescent bench test on a cold board cannot replicate the thermal reality of a warm, multi-hour operating shift.

Consider an application SoC inside this bay modeled with a lumped junction-to-ambient thermal resistance \(\JunctionAmbientThermalResistance\) and a thermal time constant11 \(\PackageThermalTimeConstant\). Under a continuous power dissipation \(\DevicePower\), the silicon junction reaches a steady-state temperature of \(\AmbientTemperature + \DevicePower \JunctionAmbientThermalResistance\). When power steps to a new level, the junction temperature converges toward this new equilibrium along a first-order exponential trajectory governed by \(\PackageThermalTimeConstant\). This time constant represents the fast pole of the die and package thermal network. While heatsink and structural chassis poles operate on timescales of minutes to hours, holding the chassis at a constant baseline temperature provides an optimistic lower bound on junction heating.

11 Thermal RC Analogy: Heat flow is mathematically equivalent to electric current, temperature difference to voltage, thermal resistance (K/W) to electrical resistance, and heat capacity (J/K) to capacitance. Under a single dominant heat path, the silicon junction behaves as a first-order RC circuit with \(\PackageThermalTimeConstant = \JunctionAmbientThermalResistance \PackageThermalCapacitance\). The parameters in this scenario imply a lumped thermal capacitance of 4 J/K, representing the stored thermal energy the package must shed through \(\JunctionAmbientThermalResistance\) before the governor can restore the clock. Much like an electrical RC low-pass filter smooths voltage spikes, this physical thermal mass integrates fast fluctuations in proposal compute power into a slow, sustained temperature rise that directly governs the permission core’s clock.

Under the clock and thermal assumptions below, these dynamics eliminate the modeled configuration of candidate (b) in table 1. In that candidate design, the permission path runs on a real-time CPU cluster located on the main application SoC. Suppose this cluster shares the SoC’s primary clock and power management domain, sized so that the permission tick’s worst-case cycle count, run at the nominal clock, exactly meets its declared worst-case execution time. The sizing is not chosen to make the case fail. The declared bound is the worst execution the placement may assume, so judging the placement at that bound is the honest test, and a tick that executes at its bound has no slack against any slowing of the clock. When sustained neural network inference drives the junction temperature beyond the trip threshold \(\ThrottleTripTemperature\), the hardware governor halves the cluster’s clock frequency. As a consequence, every instruction cycle in the tick takes twice as long. The safety software executes the exact same code, but the shared governor injects an unmodeled cross-domain delay that isolated execution time profiling cannot detect. A discrete microcontroller, in contrast, runs on an independent oscillator and dedicated power rail beyond the reach of the SoC’s thermal governor. An on-die safety island fed by its own clock tree and voltage rail would likewise isolate clock generation from the governor, although it would still share the silicon substrate and require rigorous thermal qualification.

A throttled tick outlasts the workload burst because hardware governors enforce state-dependent hysteresis. To prevent the clock frequency from chattering rapidly around the trip line, the controller restores the nominal clock only after junction temperature drops below a lower clearance threshold \(\ThrottleClearTemperature\). Once proposal inference halts and chip power falls back to baseline, the junction relaxes toward its baseline equilibrium: \[\JunctionTemperature(\TimeInstant) = \QuiescentJunctionTemperature + (\ThrottleOnsetJunctionTemperature - \QuiescentJunctionTemperature) e^{-\TimeInstant/\PackageThermalTimeConstant} \tag{4}\] where \(\ThrottleOnsetJunctionTemperature\) is the junction temperature when throttling begins, \(\QuiescentJunctionTemperature\) is the baseline equilibrium under quiescent load, and \(\PackageThermalTimeConstant\) is the package thermal time constant. Solving equation 4 for the time required to cool from \(\ThrottleTripTemperature\) to \(\ThrottleClearTemperature\) gives \(\ThrottleRecoveryTime = \PackageThermalTimeConstant \ln[(\ThrottleOnsetJunctionTemperature - \QuiescentJunctionTemperature)/(\ThrottleClearTemperature - \QuiescentJunctionTemperature)]\), which fixes how long the hangover lasts in this scenario.

Napkin Math 13.2: The thermal hangover
Problem: Candidate (b)’s permission cluster shares the application die’s package and clock governor. Under continuous proposal inference, does the junction reach the trip threshold, what does the throttled clock do to the tick, and for how many ticks does the overrun persist after the load stops?

Variables:

  • \(\AmbientTemperature =\) 45\(\,^{\circ}\text{C}\), \(\JunctionAmbientThermalResistance =\) 2 K/W, and \(\PackageThermalTimeConstant =\) 8 s (Illustrative; conduction-cooled bay).
  • \(\QuiescentSocPower =\) 4 W for the permission loop with idle and memory power, and \(\InferenceLoadSocPower =\) 26 W with continuous proposal inference (Illustrative). The scenario holds this loaded power constant after throttling; a real device whose governor reduces power requires a coupled power-and-temperature model.
  • \(\ThrottleTripTemperature =\) 95\(\,^{\circ}\text{C}\) and \(\ThrottleClearTemperature =\) 85\(\,^{\circ}\text{C}\) (Illustrative governor thresholds).
  • \(\NominalClockFrequency =\) 1.2 GHz, halved to \(\ThrottledClockFrequency =\) 600 MHz (Illustrative). The tick is sized to \(\PermissionTickWcet =\) 250 μs at \(\NominalClockFrequency\) inside the 400 μs deadline and the 1 ms tick (Chosen), with 90 μs of blocking and interference charged as in equation 4.3 (Illustrative).

Math: The steady states are \(\QuiescentJunctionTemperature =\) 45 \(+\) 4 \(\times\) 2 \(=\) 53\(\,^{\circ}\text{C}\) and \(\LoadedJunctionTemperature =\) 45 \(+\) 26 \(\times\) 2 \(=\) 97\(\,^{\circ}\text{C}\), above the trip. Rising from the baseline, the junction trips after \(\PackageThermalTimeConstant \ln[(\LoadedJunctionTemperature - \QuiescentJunctionTemperature)/(\LoadedJunctionTemperature - \ThrottleTripTemperature)] =\) 8 \(\ln[(\) 97 \(-\) 53 \()/(\) 97 \(-\) 95 \()] =\) 24.7 s. The tick needs \(\TickCycleCount = \PermissionTickWcet \NominalClockFrequency =\) 300,000 cycles, which at 600 MHz take 500 μs. With blocking and interference the response grows from 340 μs to 590 μs, 190 μs past the deadline. If the load stops just as the junction trips, equation 4 returns the clock after \(\ThrottleRecoveryTime =\) 8 \(\ln[(\) 95 \(-\) 53 \()/(\) 85 \(-\) 53 \()] =\) 2.18 s, which spans 2,175 ticks of 1 ms. If inference has run long enough for the junction to settle at \(\LoadedJunctionTemperature\), cooling starts there instead and takes 8 \(\ln[(\) 97 \(-\) 53 \()/(\) 85 \(-\) 53 \()] =\) 2.55 s, or 2,547 ticks.

Result: After about 24.7 s of continuous inference, every tick overruns its deadline by 47.5 percent for as long as inference continues, and for another 2.18 s to 2.55 s after it stops, depending on how close the junction had climbed to its loaded steady state.

Systems insight: While inference continues, the junction stays near \(\LoadedJunctionTemperature\), above the trip, so the cluster remains throttled for as long as the proposer runs, which is the worse case on a machine whose chunk policy runs continuously while it moves. The hangover is the subtler one. Pausing the proposer (at a stop, or when the policy idles) does not restore the clock for at least another 2.18 s, so a remedy that sheds proposal load cannot rescue the tick in time. Throughout, a refusal misses the drive frame timed to the deadline and waits for a later one. Because the throttled response still ends inside the period, each late refusal slips by about one tick, a distance the stopping budget can absorb (section 13.7). The case still disqualifies this modeled configuration, because the loaded tick has crossed the declared bound, every margin downstream was computed on the premise it breaks, and nothing in the premise limits a real governor to halving the clock. The lever is the heat path, not the tick’s code. Any \(\JunctionAmbientThermalResistance\) that keeps \(\InferenceLoadSocPower \JunctionAmbientThermalResistance\) below \(\ThrottleTripTemperature - \AmbientTemperature\) never trips, and an independently clocked placement is unaffected by that host governor. Its own thermal and clock limits remain separate qualification obligations.

The two timescales meet in one picture: a cooling curve measured in seconds holds the clock at 600 MHz, and every tick inside it, measured in microseconds, ends 190 μs past the deadline (figure 3). The scenario is also optimistic. Over a full shift, heat accumulating in the chassis raises the baseline \(\QuiescentJunctionTemperature\), which flattens the cooling gradient and lengthens recovery, and higher-order thermal poles in the enclosure can stretch the hysteresis by minutes. A shared-die placement would need a separately timed hardware fallback to stay safe across every exposed tick.

Heat completes the set of couplings, and placing it beside the other two shows how far apart they sit in time. The rail droop of section 13.3 acts within nanoseconds (\(10^{-9}\,\text{s}\)) of an accelerator current step, while thermal coupling develops over seconds (\(10^1\,\text{s}\)) as heat diffuses through silicon, package, and chassis. The separation is also a diagnostic: a latency spike that correlates with rail voltage points to power delivery network (PDN) impedance, and a slowdown that correlates with temperature points to the governor. Together with the tick they threaten, the couplings span about thirteen orders of magnitude on one machine, from nanoseconds to hours:

  1. \(10^{-9}\,\text{s}\) (Nanoseconds): Simultaneous tensor core switching generates \(\PackageInductance \cdot d\ConductorCurrent/d\TimeInstant\) inductive rail collapse, stretching transistor gate delays under the \(\VelocitySaturationExponent\)-power law within individual clock cycles.
  2. \(10^{-6}\,\text{s}\) (Microseconds): AXI4 crossbar arbitration and head-of-line FIFO queueing stall memory reads, as row-buffer precharge and activation penalties (\(\RowPrechargeTime + \RowActivationDelay\)) of tens of nanoseconds each accumulate across a queue.
  3. \(10^{-4}\text{--}10^{-3}\,\text{s}\) (Sub-milliseconds to milliseconds): The protected work itself. Sensor ingestion and the control barrier filter’s quadratic program run inside the permission tick, and fieldbus frames (such as EtherCAT) repeat on deterministic 1 ms cycles.
  4. \(10^0\text{--}10^1\,\text{s}\) (Seconds): Macroscopic silicon die and package heatsink thermal capacitance stores dissipated energy, enforcing governor throttling hysteresis (at least 2.18 s in this scenario) that degrades thousands of control ticks.
  5. \(10^2\text{--}10^4\,\text{s}\) (Minutes to hours): Structural chassis thermal mass and battery Joule heating accumulate heat, shifting bay ambient temperature \(\AmbientTemperature\) upward and eroding cooling headroom across a multi-hour shift.

Coupling on one chassis spans nanoseconds to seconds.

Brooks, David, and Margaret Martonosi. 2001. “Dynamic Thermal Management for High-Performance Microprocessors.” IEEE International Symposium on High-Performance Computer Architecture (HPCA), 171–82. https://doi.org/10.1109/HPCA.2001.903261.

Because thermal mass and heat dissipation operate across seconds and hours, dynamic thermal management (DTM) (Brooks and Martonosi 2001) and reactive thread migration cannot restore a co-located permission loop before its next deadline expires. A safety-critical permission path requires guaranteed lower bounds on clock frequency and power supply integrity that survive sustained accelerator workloads. When a shared die cannot guarantee them, moving the permission engine onto an independently clocked and independently powered microcontroller removes the host-governor coupling, though the microcontroller must still stay within its own qualified supply, clock, and thermal limits under the common chassis conditions.

Checkpoint 13.2: Junction temperature and clock throttling

The cooldown and overrun in figure 3 follow from the stipulated thermal scenario, not from measured hardware behavior. Use them to check the consequences of the selected model:

13.5 The Boundary Contract

Relocating the permission path isolates the execution domains on separate processors, but the two still have to talk. Consider an upstream proposal writer, such as the chunk policy on the application processor, that stalls or crashes halfway through publishing an action chunk. The downstream safety enforcer running on the real-time microcontroller still owes the physical plant a deterministic actuation decision on its very next tick. Partitioning the compute engines isolates them only if the protocol lets the real-time reader inspect and reject an incomplete or in-flight record without ever waiting for the producer. The boundary contract therefore ties memory consistency directly to the deadline of the sensing and constraint-checking pipeline, and it is the one piece of the design that every placement, discrete or shared, must get right.

13.5.1 Immutable record mailboxes and non-blocking synchronization

Passing raw pointers into an application processor’s working buffer or coordinating across a blocking lock lets a slow or crashed planner expose the real-time reader to torn data structures, stale cache lines, or unbounded waits. To prevent those stalls, the boundary contract demands an immutable, self-contained record whose publication integrity the safety enforcer can verify in constant time. The proposal header and seqlock mechanism fix the message structure and publication rules, and workload placement adds the requirement to verify on the hardware that this channel meets its execution budget even when the interconnect is saturated.

Across heterogeneous silicon or separate physical chips, communication splits into two stages: link transport across the board and local publication inside the microcontroller’s memory domain.
