An ingress peripheral or DMA engine receives the payload over a physical link (SPI, PCIe, or Ethernet) and stages it into local memory. Where the target allows peripheral access to tightly coupled memory, this may be the mailbox directly; otherwise a bounded local copy publishes the received record into the mailbox. Link transport and local publication are therefore two separate timing obligations, and the boundary protocol governs only the second.

Inside that mailbox, the receive handler publishes fixed-size records with the single-attempt seqlock from the Nervous System, which discards a torn or in-flight record for this tick without a retry. Placement adds two obligations that the protocol alone does not discharge. Memory attributes, barriers, and cache maintenance on the target must keep the local copy coherent, and the copy itself, at its largest payload, must fit inside the tick’s declared WCET.

↩︎ The single-attempt seqlock protocol, whose reader never spins or retries within a tick, is established in Lock-free and wait-free mailbox exchanges.

Memory consistency shows only that a proposal arrived without tearing, not that the command is safe to execute. Once the record passes sequence validation, the admission pipeline checks its algorithmic certificates, dynamic feasibility, transport integrity, and lease validity. Rejecting a corrupted or late proposal neither renews an expiring lease nor revokes an active, previously vetted command early. The actuator keeps executing the current plan while that plan’s lease, kinematic bounds, and state checks hold. If the current plan expires or the machine leaves its safe operating bounds, the microcontroller invokes the fallback ladder immediately. Host-side operating-system stalls, neural accelerator throttling, and asynchronous logging queues can never backpressure or delay this local decision.

Every cross-boundary exchange therefore carries two separately measured costs: board-level physical transport before publication, followed by a single bounded local read during the enforcement tick. Qualification test suites must record payload sizes, cache and memory settings, and worst-case rejection paths. Even a real-time enforcer that reads without blocking remains vulnerable if its own execution stalls, so software decoupling must be paired with independent hardware supervision of processor liveness.

13.5.2 Windowed watchdog timers and execution liveness

A standard countdown watchdog refreshed from a timer interrupt keeps getting fed after the control task has hung (Barr 2013). A single late-only threshold cannot catch a corrupted program counter or a runaway phase-locked loop (PLL) clock that jumps into the watchdog service routine ahead of schedule. The single-attempt seqlock catches torn publication, and memory protection blocks cross-domain writes, but neither verifies that the safety enforcer software is alive and running its checks at the intended rate. The Nervous System feeds the microcontroller’s self-watchdog only when a tick finishes all of its checks, which catches a late or missing feed. On shared or heterogeneous silicon, the enforcer can fail in either direction: bus contention and memory starvation can stall its progress, while PLL clock glitches or control-flow corruption can make instructions run too early. Protecting the plant therefore requires a two-sided bound on watchdog servicing.

Barr, Michael. 2013. Bookout v. Toyota Motor Corp.: 2005 Camry L4 Software Analysis.

↩︎ The supervisory timers, from the chunk lease to the self-watchdog timeout, are established in Supervisory timers and proposal wire contracts.

High-integrity physical AI systems enforce this discipline with windowed watchdog timers. Unlike standard countdown timers that catch only late feeds, a windowed watchdog enforces both a lower threshold (the closed window, \(\WatchdogEarliestServiceTime\)) and an upper threshold (the open window, \(\WatchdogLatestServiceTime\)), rejecting any refresh that arrives too early or too late:

  1. Early-kick violation (\(\WatchdogServiceTime < \WatchdogEarliestServiceTime\)): If software refreshes the watchdog before the lower threshold has elapsed, the hardware watchdog controller flags an immediate fault and trips the emergency safe state. An early refresh signals disrupted control flow: a corrupted program counter may have skipped safety barrier checks to branch straight into the housekeeping routine, a stack overflow may have aborted intermediate kinematic checks, or a PLL multiplier glitch may have sped up the clock. Because a compromised execution path could still happen to feed the timer inside the valid window, primary hardware monitors (independent clock monitors and rail supervisors with isolated voltage references) must catch PLL runaways and supply dips first, leaving the windowed watchdog as a backstop.
  2. Late-kick violation (\(\WatchdogServiceTime > \WatchdogLatestServiceTime\)): If the timer counts past \(\WatchdogLatestServiceTime\) without a valid refresh from the enforcer, the counter underflows and asserts an unmaskable hardware reset line. A late kick shows that the safety enforcer stalled, whether starved of bus cycles by neural accelerator traffic on a shared crossbar, deadlocked in an unhandled exception handler, or delayed by cache evictions.
  3. Valid service window (\(\WatchdogEarliestServiceTime \le \WatchdogServiceTime \le \WatchdogLatestServiceTime\)): A refresh inside this interval confirms only that the service call arrived on time. It does not prove that the software ran every required check in the dispatch loop. Full diagnostic coverage combines windowed timing with challenge-response checks or independent hardware supervision.

If the controller feeds once after each successful tick, the watchdog measures the interval between feeds. Let completion offset \(\CompletionOffset_n\) from tick \(\TickIndex\) start lie in \([\MinimumCompletionOffset,\MaximumCompletionOffset]\), and let \(\TickPeriodJitterBound\) bound the deviation of the interval between consecutive tick starts from \(\NominalTickPeriod\). A conservative range for the inter-feed interval is given by equation 5: \[\InterFeedInterval\in[\NominalTickPeriod-(\MaximumCompletionOffset-\MinimumCompletionOffset)-\TickPeriodJitterBound,\;\NominalTickPeriod+(\MaximumCompletionOffset-\MinimumCompletionOffset)+\TickPeriodJitterBound] \tag{5}\] This formulation captures the two extreme consecutive tick completions:

  • Shortest interval (late-to-early compression): Tick \(\TickIndex\) finishes as late as possible (\(\MaximumCompletionOffset\)), and Tick \(\TickIndex+1\) finishes as early as possible (\(\MinimumCompletionOffset\)); the elapsed time between consecutive feeds is compressed to \(\NominalTickPeriod - (\MaximumCompletionOffset - \MinimumCompletionOffset) - \TickPeriodJitterBound\).
  • Longest interval (early-to-late expansion): Tick \(\TickIndex\) finishes as early as possible (\(\MinimumCompletionOffset\)), and Tick \(\TickIndex+1\) finishes as late as possible (\(\MaximumCompletionOffset\)); the elapsed time between consecutive feeds expands to \(\NominalTickPeriod + (\MaximumCompletionOffset - \MinimumCompletionOffset) + \TickPeriodJitterBound\).

Engineers set \(\WatchdogEarliestServiceTime\) and \(\WatchdogLatestServiceTime\) around this range once timing analysis and test evidence justify its bounds, while keeping the late-trip threshold inside the plant’s fault budget. A single tick’s nominal execution time is not enough to set a watchdog window. On the running machine, \(\NominalTickPeriod\) is the 1 ms tick, and the declared worst-case execution time of 250 μs gives the execution contribution to \(\MaximumCompletionOffset\). The completion offset must also include the internal blocking and interference charged to the tick, so both offset bounds must use the same tick-start reference. Conversely, the nominal unloaded measurement of 135 μs cannot serve as \(\MinimumCompletionOffset\); early-exit paths, short branches, or trivial constraint checks can finish much faster than nominal paths. The shortest tick-start-to-completion interval observed during a stress and fault-injection campaign gives evidence for \(\MinimumCompletionOffset\), and a justified lower bound must also cover healthy paths the campaign did not exercise. Similarly, measured cycle-to-cycle period jitter under peak bus contention supports the bound \(\TickPeriodJitterBound\), with allowances for unobserved conditions, rather than an idealized absolute phase offset. If a team has only absolute phase bounds, it must take the maximum difference between consecutive phase offsets before using this expression.

The two ends of the window are set by different arguments. The lower end of equation 5 sets \(\WatchdogEarliestServiceTime\). Once \(\MinimumCompletionOffset\) and \(\TickPeriodJitterBound\) have justified bounds, supported by a campaign and allowances for unobserved healthy paths, any feed earlier than the resulting lower bound lies outside the qualified healthy range. This gives the machine’s self-watchdog, which has only a late trip, the early trip it lacks. The upper end is not used as \(\WatchdogLatestServiceTime\). It marks the latest a healthy tick can feed, so \(\WatchdogLatestServiceTime\) must sit at or beyond it. How far beyond is a separate choice of fault-detection latency, judged against what the plant can tolerate while a dead enforcer goes undetected. The machine keeps the selected 5 ms timeout, several ticks beyond the window, so that transient delays do not trigger spurious resets; problem 5 converts that timeout into base travel. The late trip also answers a different question from the falsification threshold. A tick that runs past the declared WCET but still feeds within the window refutes the placement’s timing premise, and the loaded campaign, not the watchdog, must catch it. The watchdog’s upper end catches only an enforcer that has stopped feeding altogether.

Setting a multi-tick timeout is an engineered fault-detection latency, not proof that missing several control cycles is harmless. If the microcontroller crashes or resets, it cannot run MCU-controlled powered deceleration. A drive-autonomous SS1 remains available only if its independent trigger, drive controller, supply, and drive-energy and regenerative-sink paths survive the fault and are qualified for that response. Otherwise, the platform falls back to its separately qualified passive braking, friction deceleration, or hardwired mechanical interlocks. Total fault-handling latency (watchdog trip delay, supervisory reset propagation, and brake engagement) must therefore fit inside the plant’s certified safety-stop budget. Engineers cannot justify a slow watchdog timeout by citing powered braking distances when the fault being detected disables the processor that commands those brakes.

To catch control-flow corruption where a broken loop still passes through the watchdog service call inside the valid time window, high-integrity designs add challenge-response checkpointing. An external hardware supervisor or isolated safety coprocessor sends pseudo-random seeds to the enforcer at runtime. Instead of writing a fixed value to a register, the enforcer must return a signature computed from fresh sensor frame hashes, constraint-barrier checks, and intermediate trajectory stages. Diagnostic coverage depends on whether a corrupted execution path can replay or compute a valid signature by chance. If the enforcer fails the challenge or misses the window, the external supervisor asserts a hardware torque-inhibit line (such as Safe Torque Off on an electric drive). Direct torque removal stops actuators from driving into hazards, but it is not a controlled powered stop; the resulting passive coast-down or mechanical braking profile needs its own qualification and safety-case evidence.

Both the boundary contract and its hardware supervision apply to every workload placement. Whether the learned policy and the real-time safety enforcer sit on separate boards, separate chiplets, or partitioned cores on one SoC, the design must pass records without blocking and verify liveness in hardware. What changes from one placement to another is how much cross-domain interference the tick must absorb, and every mechanism that bounds that interference carries its own price in silicon area, bus bandwidth, and board complexity.

13.6 Silicon Isolation Decisions

Suppose a loaded test catches one of these couplings, a tick that exceeds the declared bound under proposal load. The team then has four remedies, each paid for in a different currency: static reservation, spatial partitioning, workload reduction, and physical relocation to separate silicon (table 3). Each remedy targets a specific coupling layer and leaves a residual vulnerability; when that residual interference still exceeds the timing or fault-containment bound, the design must escalate to the next row, culminating in the mobile manipulator’s choice of physical relocation and the board-link transport budget it requires.

13.6.1 Four remedies for shared-silicon contention

A remedy that does not match the active coupling channel leaves the deadline overrun intact and strands compute capacity. Examples include reserving CPU cores when the stall lives in the DRAM controller queue, and coloring cache sets while miss status holding registers remain shared. A queueing bottleneck requires an enforced bound on service time, a cache capacity conflict requires physical separation of working sets, and a power-supply or clock disturbance requires an isolated fault domain. Trimming proposal demand can recover operating headroom, but only a bound on worst-case residual interference can support a safety claim.

Under static reservation,12 physical workload placement stays the same while the critical path gets dedicated CPU time slices or interconnect slots. Reserving CPU time alone does not bound memory access latency; the interconnect needs its own service guarantee, such as periodic time-division multiple access (TDMA) slots with fixed transaction beat quotas. When hardware crossbars lack slot arbitration, system software must step in. Memory-bandwidth regulation gives each core an access budget per regulation epoch, counts memory transactions with hardware performance counters, and stalls any aggressor core with an interrupt once it spends its quota until the next epoch begins (Yun et al. 2013). By capping how much traffic an aggressor can queue ahead of a critical transaction, bandwidth regulation provides a demand bound from which the controller model and service guarantee can bound the queueing multiplier \(\InterferenceSlowdownFactor\) of section 13.3.2. Throttling reads alone is not enough; defending against writeback-buffer saturation requires charging both reads and dirty-line writebacks against the aggressor’s budget (Bechtel and Yun 2019). The predictable execution model takes this separation further on the real-time core. It splits each task into an initial memory phase that pulls its working set into private scratchpad memory, followed by an isolated compute phase that touches no shared interconnect resources (Pellizzoni et al. 2011). That pattern matches the boundary contract of section 13.5, which performs one bounded read followed by local computation. The cost is stranded capacity, because memory throughput stays reserved for the safety path even when the enforcer is idle. Had the warehouse mobile manipulator kept its permission path on the SoC, reserving memory-bus slots for sporadic 4 KB sensor reads would throttle policy weight streaming across the accelerator even with the robot base parked.

12 Time Partitioning (TDMA): Time-division multiple access gives each requester a fixed recurring slot, so its worst wait is one schedule cycle whatever the others demand. Integrated modular avionics made the same idea the basis of processor sharing: the ARINC 653 interface schedules partitions in fixed windows of a repeating major frame, and the assurance argument for such partitioning treats spatial and temporal separation as distinct obligations (Rushby 1999). The price is capacity reserved even when its owner is idle, the cost this paragraph charges to the proposer.

Rushby, John. 1999. Partitioning in Avionics Architectures: Requirements, Mechanisms, and Assurance. NASA/CR-1999-209347. NASA Langley Research Center.
Mancuso, Renato, Roman Dudko, Emiliano Betti, Marco Cesati, Marco Caccamo, and Rodolfo Pellizzoni. 2013. “Real-Time Cache Management Framework for Multi-Core Architectures.” IEEE Real-Time and Embedded Technology and Applications Symposium (RTAS), 45–54. https://doi.org/10.1109/RTAS.2013.6531078.
The Linux Kernel Documentation. 2026. MPAM.

When contention comes from competing working sets thrashing shared caches or banks, spatial partitioning isolates cache ways or physical memory channels. For example, splitting an 8 MB last-level cache into 6 MB for the proposal policy and 2 MB for enforcer permission tables prevents cross-domain cache-line evictions, though shrinking the perception working set can increase off-chip memory traffic. Operating systems can enforce this split without special hardware through cache coloring: the page allocator assigns physical pages whose index bits map to disjoint cache sets (Mancuso et al. 2013). Extending address-bit coloring to the bits that select DRAM banks gives each domain private banks, removing the inter-core row-buffer conflicts of equation 1. Arm’s Memory System Resource Partitioning and Monitoring (MPAM) extension puts these controls in hardware, combining cache-portion bitmaps across shared caches with memory-bandwidth throttling registers downstream of the last-level cache (The Linux Kernel Documentation 2026). Even so, spatial cache partitioning does not separate the shared MSHRs or writeback queues (section 13.3.2). Spatial partitioning must therefore be paired with bandwidth regulation, and neither removes a shared DRAM controller queue. Giving the safety path its own DRAM channel and controller removes controller contention, at the cost of permanently taking that channel’s bandwidth away from the proposal accelerator.

A static-partitioning hypervisor combines these mechanisms across one SoC. At boot, it statically binds CPU cores, physical memory ranges, and memory-mapped peripherals to isolated guest partitions, runs without a dynamic runtime scheduler, and colors cache sets across partitions, as in microkernels such as Bao (Martins et al. 2020). This is the standard way to host a hard real-time safety enclave beside a Linux stack on one die. The scheduling model behind the asymmetry between proposal overruns and enforcer guarantees is mixed-criticality scheduling (Vestal 2007; Burns and Davis 2017). In the standard mixed-criticality model, execution budgets depend on the assurance level: if a high-criticality task overruns its lower-assurance budget, the scheduler drops low-criticality work so the high-criticality task keeps its larger budget. On the running machine, the safety enforcer is the high-criticality task and the generative proposer is the low-criticality workload. The lease protocol enforces the same priority: a proposer that misses its inference window forfeits lease renewal, while qualified hardware isolation protects the enforcer’s periodic permission tick.

Martins, José, Adriano Tavares, Marco Solieri, Marko Bertogna, and Sandro Pinto. 2020. “Bao: A Lightweight Static Partitioning Hypervisor for Modern Multi-Core Embedded Systems.” Workshop on Next Generation Real-Time Embedded Systems (NG-RES), OASIcs, vol. 77: 3:1–14. https://doi.org/10.4230/OASIcs.NG-RES.2020.3.

When an application can accept coarser tracking, workload reduction lowers memory traffic and heat through model quantization, smaller intermediate tensor transfers, pruned telemetry logging, or a lower policy rate. The trade-off is tracking fidelity. On the mobile manipulator, stretching the action-chunk period past the 60 ms lease lets leases expire between renewals; keeping continuous control authority requires chunk admissions above 16.7 Hz, even before renewal jitter is charged. Average producer throughput cannot verify this condition. Post-training quantization also requires fresh testing to confirm that manipulation accuracy stays within tolerance. Finally, lowering average power does not remove fast current steps (\(\FiniteChange \ConductorCurrent\)) or inductive voltage droop (\(\PackageInductance \cdot d\ConductorCurrent/d\TimeInstant\)), which remain separate electrical qualification items.

When on-chip software and hardware controls cannot bound shared-die interference, physical relocation moves the permission path to a dedicated microcontroller with private SRAM, its own crystal oscillator, a separate reset domain, and an isolated power supply. Private TCM removes DRAM bus contention, while an isolated rail with hold-up capacitance keeps the safety monitor alive through host-side voltage collapse. Relocation does not remove verification work; it trades on-die contention analysis for board-level signal integrity, transceiver qualification, and common-cause electrical fault containment.

Moving the permission enforcer to separate silicon shifts the cost from on-chip arbitration to board-level link latency and PCB routing. The drive command bus delay (\(\CommandTransportTime\)) does not change because joint drives sit on an EtherCAT fieldbus regardless of compute placement. What relocation adds is proposal transport across an inter-chip link (a DMA-driven SPI link, an isolated PCIe Non-Transparent Bridge, or a differential serial bus) from the application processor to the safety microcontroller. While this board hop does not delay an enforcer tick running on already admitted evidence, it ages the observation behind every newly admitted plan and must be charged to the dispatch-to-admission interval (section 13.6.2). Jitter in transport and admission also spends the 5 ms renewal-jitter margin that the lease rule reserves between the 50 ms chunk period and the 60 ms lease. Host-side memory contention spends the same margin: an illustrative 12.5 percent stretch of the inference uses up the entire jitter allowance by itself, leaving nothing for link delays or validation variance. Lease renewal must therefore be checked against measured end-to-end admission timestamps rather than upstream inference rates.

↩︎ The renewal-jitter margin and lease renewal rule are established in Chunk Lease Sizing from the Stopping Budget.

Physical relocation also changes where sensors attach. On the running machine, joint encoders and the IMU wire directly to peripheral controllers on the safety microcontroller (hardware quadrature counters and synchronous SPI channels), keeping host-processor arbitration out of the real-time feedback loop. Driver latency and register-read jitter on those peripherals still need measurement. By contrast, high-bandwidth perception sensors feed the application processor directly: RGB cameras over MIPI CSI-2 lanes, and lidar packets over Ethernet. Those heavy streams fill perception buffers without crossing the microcontroller’s internal buses. Every placement choice thus reshapes the board, trading on-die memory arbitration for pin counts, differential trace routing, and serial link overhead.

13.6.2 The board-link budget and the dispatch-to-admission interval

That board-level serial hop and split validation pipeline age sensor observations while the base keeps rolling down the aisle, closing the interval left unallocated in The latency waterfall. This span, from perception dispatching an observation frame to the safety enforcer admitting the resulting action chunk, is called the dispatch-to-admission interval \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\) (the second row of The capture-to-brake-onset timing budget of the running machine). It covers the remaining proposer inference after dispatch, serialization across the board link, frame receipt and deserialization on the microcontroller, host geometry checks and bounded local admission checking, trajectory buffer activation, and the wait for the next permission tick. Because the stopping budget starts at sensor capture, every millisecond of \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\) is base travel on aging evidence, paid for out of the stopping budget’s spare clearance.

↩︎ The composite stopping budget, which leaves the dispatch-to-admission interval out of the pre-brake delay and pays for it from the remaining clearance, is established in Sizing the composite stopping budget.

Napkin Math 13.3: The board-link toll
Problem: The machine’s permission path runs on a discrete microcontroller that receives each chunk over a board link. How long may the interval from perception dispatch to admission last before the finished stopping budget has nothing left at the aisle speed?

Variables:

  • Chunk policy p99 inference 40 ms (Illustrative), charged in full after dispatch, which is conservative because its vision stage already lies in the dispatch age of The latency waterfall.
  • Serialized chunk \(\SerializedChunkVolume =\) 4,096 bytes on a \(\SerialLinkClockFrequency =\) 25 MHz SPI link (Illustrative).
  • Deserialization 2 ms, aggregate trajectory validation 18 ms, and activation 1 ms (Illustrative allowances), and up to one 1 ms permission tick of waiting (Chosen).
  • At \(\SpeedAtDetection =\) 1.3 m/s the finished budget leaves \(\SpareStoppingClearance =\) 131.0 mm (Derived).

The validation allowance covers both the host geometry check and the MCU’s bounded local admission check, including their scheduling delays. The example does not allocate a separate duration to each. Their combined elapsed contribution must fit this allowance; qualification cannot omit host validation or count the entire allowance again on the MCU.

Math: Serialization takes \(8\,\SerializedChunkVolume/\SerialLinkClockFrequency =\) 1.31 ms. The allocation totals \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval =\) 40 ms \(+\) 1.31 ms \(+\) 2 ms \(+\) 18 ms \(+\) 1 ms \(+\) 1 ms \(=\) 63.3 ms, which the base covers as 82.3 mm of travel. The spare pays for at most \(\DispatchAdmissionCeiling = \SpareStoppingClearance/\SpeedAtDetection =\) 131.0 mm \(/\) 1.3 m/s \(=\) 100.8 ms, so the allocation leaves 37.5 ms, or 48.7 mm of positive reserve. Doubling the chunk or halving the link clock adds only another 1.31 ms; adding one more inference’s worth of delay, 103.3 ms in all, exhausts the spare.

Systems insight: The board link that buys silicon separation costs about one millisecond, a small fraction of \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\); proposer inference and aggregate trajectory validation dominate the interval. The stipulated discrete-placement allocation therefore fits the stopping budget, whereas a slower policy or a heavier validation kernel could exhaust it. The allocation is a target that qualification must confirm, not a relaxed runtime margin. The deterministic portion of \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\) ages observation evidence and is charged here, while runtime variance between renewals drains the 5 ms renewal-jitter budget, which host-side memory contention alone can saturate. Because host inference and local validation overlap as a pipeline across consecutive 50 ms chunk periods, a 63.3 ms dispatch-to-admission interval does not expire the 60 ms lease as long as the time between consecutive admissions stays within the chunk period plus that renewal-jitter allowance.

With \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\) formally allocated, the event timing table (The capture-to-brake-onset timing budget of the running machine) contains no undefined intervals. Total plan replacement latency from sensor capture to chunk admission (equation 11.3) is 114.9 ms, while total elapsed time from capture to emergency brake onset in the event of an unresponsive proposer is 196.9 ms. Both figures represent budget allocations that the loaded stress-testing campaign in section 13.7 must validate using physical hardware trace timestamps.

The board link costs 1.31 ms of a 63.3 ms allocation under a 100.8 ms ceiling.

Allocating the interval also exposes interference that the discrete placement keeps inside the microcontroller. The link’s receive DMA engine and its completion interrupt, the fieldbus master dispatching motor drive frames, background local admission checking, and the periodic ingestion of evidence snapshots all contend for the microcontroller’s internal crossbar, interrupt controller, and memory banks. Every potential interference path demands an enforceable bound: assigning SPI and fieldbus interrupt priorities strictly below the deterministic permission tick, allocating dedicated DMA channels, and mapping safety state to independent tightly coupled memory banks. The loaded validation campaign in section 13.7 must saturate the communication link and execute maximum-length plan validation while simultaneously triggering the refusal branch. Classical response-time analysis for the periodic tick, with its higher-priority preemption and lower-priority blocking terms, provides the formal static counterpart to those empirical stress tests.

↪ Response-time analysis for a periodic tick, with its preemption and blocking terms, is developed in Real-Time Timers, Watchdogs, and Schedulability.

These remediation pathways determine which computing resources engineers must statically reserve or physically isolate. Two subsequent architectural decisions govern the remaining placement budget: whether any portion of proposal generation may be offloaded across an external network, and which candidate accelerator silicon completes autonomous missions within the chassis energy reserve. Off-board compute is first a timing question, and it comes first because energy efficiency matters only among architectures that already meet their deadlines.

13.6.3 Off-board compute and mission energy

Querying a remote data center over factory Wi-Fi or private 5G subjects an offloaded policy to radio multipath fading, base-station handoffs, and packet retransmissions, yet the returned trajectory must still arrive inside the local 55 ms lease-renewal window. Off-board computation operates under the exact same timing windows as a local board interconnect, so an offloaded result must fit the target admission window before it can be considered for admission: \[\OffloadTailNetworkTime + \OffloadTailProcessingTime \le \OffloadTransactionWindow, \tag{6}\] where \(\OffloadTransactionWindow\) is the allocation for the network-and-remote-processing interval of a particular transaction. The complete dispatch-to-admission interval must also charge host validation, local deserialization and admission, activation, and tick waiting against the 100.8 ms ceiling (notebook 13.3). A continuing pipeline must satisfy that evidence-age ceiling and the accepted-result gap required for lease renewal. The lease less renewal jitter (55 ms on the running machine) directly limits transaction latency only under a serial, non-overlapping convention; with overlapping requests, stage capacity and actual inter-admission gaps require separate checks. Qualification must measure the complete end-to-end tail at the required reliability level and retain the same evidence, state, and lease checks as local admission.

Empirical robotics deployments demonstrate how severely this constraint restricts offloading. In RT-2, large vision-language-action (VLA) models ran in a multi-TPU data center queried over wireless networks; under real network conditions, the 55-billion-parameter model executed at only 1 to 3 Hz, while the 5-billion-parameter model reached approximately 5 Hz (Brohan et al. 2023). Wireless links in industrial deployments (whether Wi-Fi or private 5G) introduce severe latency tails from radio-frequency multipath fading, base-station handoffs, and packet retransmissions. At 5 Hz, a fresh plan arrives only once every 200 ms, 3.6 times the renewal window, while the 55-billion-parameter model delivers outputs at best every 333.3 ms. Neither remote pipeline could sustain lease renewal on this machine. Because the published study documents overall throughput frequencies rather than an explicit breakdown between network latency and remote execution phases, one cannot determine whether a lower-latency wireless link could close the gap. Qualifying any remote lease-renewal pipeline requires profiling the full tail distributions of both components. Instead, large remote foundation models are best positioned to synthesize coarse semantic goals or task decompositions, leaving high-frequency action chunk generation to an onboard policy. Coarser planning workloads operating on second-scale horizons are better candidates for edge offloading. FogROS2, for example, offloads ROS 2 compute graphs to cloud and fog instances, reducing grasp-planning latency from 14 s on local edge silicon to 1.2 s (Ichnowski et al. 2023). That acceleration speeds macro-task completion while leaving high-frequency microcontroller execution untouched. Packet loss or complete link disconnection must leave the onboard enforcer and its certified safe-stop routine fully operational. Neither remote offloading nor offline compilation eliminates the obligation to guarantee timely execution on local silicon.

Brohan, Anthony, Noah Brown, Justice Carbajal, Yevgen Chebotar, Xi Chen, Krzysztof Choromanski, Tianli Ding, et al. 2023. “RT-2: Vision-Language-Action Models Transfer Web Knowledge to Robotic Control.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2307.15818.
Ichnowski, Jeffrey, Kaiyuan Chen, Karthik Dharmarajan, Simeon Adebola, Michael Danielczuk, Víctor Mayoral-Vilches, Nikhil Jha, et al. 2023. “FogROS2: An Adaptive Platform for Cloud and Fog Robotics Using ROS 2.” IEEE International Conference on Robotics and Automation (ICRA), 5493–500. https://doi.org/10.1109/ICRA48891.2023.10161307.

Placement also decides which accelerator hosts the proposal path, and that choice reaches the permission path through energy as well as time. The chip’s power is the load term that set the junction temperature in section 13.4, and it draws on the same battery whose sag the isolated rail must ride through. Among architectures that satisfy these timing bounds, candidate silicon must also complete the physical mission within the chassis energy reserve. Energy optimization often presents a subtle engineering trade-off. A lower-power edge accelerator draws fewer watts during active inference, yet if it extends execution latency, peripheral loads (lidar, depth cameras, cooling fans, and motor holding currents) remain energized longer. Conversely, a higher-throughput neural accelerator reduces mission energy only as long as faster compute actively shortens overall task duration. Once physical manipulator kinematics or vehicle velocity limits dominate the execution timeline, accelerating compute further merely burns excess power without saving task time, driving up total energy consumed per completed cycle.

For an assigned physical workload with steady-state compute power and baseline chassis power draw, total mission energy follows the relation: \[\MissionEnergy = (\ComputeChipPower + \StaticChassisPower) \times \MissionDuration\] Evaluating this expression requires an empirical or rigorously modeled mission duration \(\MissionDuration\) for each candidate silicon configuration. Transient motor acceleration spikes, DC-DC converter losses, task retries, and quiescent dwell intervals demand explicit energy accounting whenever they diverge across platforms. Systems engineers cannot rank candidate SoCs simply by comparing datasheet figures such as peak tera-operations per second (TOPS) or nominal thermal design power (TDP). The workload to measure is the machine’s own proposal pipeline, from camera ingestion through the chunk policy, and final silicon placement requires evaluating the compiled neural graph, its memory footprint, and the thermal assembly as one system. This chapter does not rank accelerators for the running machine, whose record carries no measured mission duration for any candidate; the relation states what such a ranking must measure. With reserved memory bandwidth, board-level transport latency, and battery energy draw accounted for in the allocation model, the next step is experimental verification. It tests on the physical hardware whether these isolation choices keep the permission tick within its bound and preserve braking response under worst-case concurrent load.

13.7 Empirical Verification of Silicon Isolation

The thermal hangover refuted candidate (b) from a model, but a placement that survives every model still has to face a measurement that could refute it. A claim of silicon isolation between an unverified learned workload and a safety enforcer must therefore reduce to a testable bound on enforcement latency and refusal availability. This bound must hold across all declared proposal workloads, junction temperatures, and operational modes. If shared-resource contention delays an enforcer refusal past its physical deadline, the claimed operational separation has failed, whatever architectural isolation was promised on paper.

13.7.1 The worst-case execution time falsification threshold

When a loaded permission tick runs for 260 µs against a declared isolated worst-case execution time of 250 μs, it finishes well inside the 400 μs deadline yet still refutes the claim that shared hardware added zero interference. The budget section of section 13.2 introduced the permission tick’s declared worst-case execution time (\(\PermissionTickWcet\)) as this falsification threshold, and a test designed to refute a placement must name the exact measured quantity it compares against that bound. In this example, the declared worst-case execution time bounds the task in isolation, whereas a qualified execution model can instead include bounded resource interference. Here, microarchitectural interference from hardware shared with co-running workloads lengthens the execution term rather than appearing as a separate term of the response-time bound (equation 4.3). The loaded \(\WorstCaseExecutionTime_i\) includes that penalty, beside the blocking \(\TaskBlockingTime_i\) and interference \(\TaskInterferenceTime_i\) that the microcontroller’s own tasks contribute.

The pass-fail threshold for silicon isolation derives directly from the system specification. The machine’s permission path specifies a declared worst-case execution time \(\PermissionTickWcet =\) 250 μs, representing the isolated execution parameter \(\WorstCaseExecutionTime_i\) in equation 4.3. It must complete each permission evaluation within a chosen enforcer deadline \(\EnforcerDeadline =\) 400 μs, which is nested inside the overall permission period \(\PermissionLoopPeriod =\) 1 ms. When measured under operational load, total enforcer execution time is the sum of isolated execution latency and cross-workload hardware interference, as formalized in equation 7: \[\LoadedEnforcerExecutionTime = \IsolatedEnforcerExecutionTime + \SharedHardwareInterferenceDelay, \qquad \IsolatedEnforcerExecutionTime \le \PermissionTickWcet < \EnforcerDeadline < \PermissionLoopPeriod \tag{7}\] where \(\IsolatedEnforcerExecutionTime\) represents the execution time of the permission path running with zero contention, and \(\SharedHardwareInterferenceDelay\) captures the cumulative delay inflicted by proposal-side activity competing for shared DRAM channels, caches, interconnects, clock trees, power distribution networks, and thermal budgets.

An ideal isolated placement claims that proposal-side execution introduces zero cross-workload interference (\(\SharedHardwareInterferenceDelay = 0\)), making the declared bound \(\PermissionTickWcet\) the falsification threshold. Because isolated execution cannot exceed \(\PermissionTickWcet\) by definition, any measured cycle under load that exceeds \(\PermissionTickWcet\) demonstrates that either the declared baseline WCET was underestimated or the shared hardware introduced an interference penalty that the architecture failed to eliminate. Either outcome refutes the timing contract of the placement, even if the cycle completes before \(\EnforcerDeadline\), because every downstream margin, from the deadline itself to the stopping distance budgeted for each tick, depends on that premise. Conversely, observing a loaded execution cycle below \(\PermissionTickWcet\) does not establish that \(\SharedHardwareInterferenceDelay\) is zero. Empirical testing can decisively falsify a declared bound, but measurements alone cannot prove its universal validity. Any test campaign samples only the microarchitectural states it reaches, and microarchitectural timing anomalies can push the true worst-case latency beyond every observed cycle.13

13 Microarchitectural Timing Anomalies: A local speedup, such as an unexpected cache hit, can reorder subsequent pipeline or interconnect resource contention and paradoxically lengthen total execution time (Lundqvist and Stenström 1999). Although first identified in dynamically scheduled out-of-order processors, timing anomalies also appear in pipelined in-order cores coupled to shared buses or caches (Wilhelm et al. 2008). Consequently, composing local worst-case assumptions at each pipeline stage does not guarantee the global worst-case execution time, and empirical observation on an in-order core cannot substitute for formal timing analysis.

Lundqvist, Thomas, and Per Stenström. 1999. “Timing Anomalies in Dynamically Scheduled Microprocessors.” IEEE Real-Time Systems Symposium (RTSS), 12–21. https://doi.org/10.1109/REAL.1999.818824.
Wilhelm, Reinhard, Jakob Engblom, Andreas Ermedahl, Niklas Holsti, Stephan Thesing, David Whalley, Guillem Bernat, et al. 2008. “The Worst-Case Execution-Time Problem: Overview of Methods and Survey of Tools.” ACM Transactions on Embedded Computing Systems 7 (3): 36:1–53. https://doi.org/10.1145/1347375.1347389.

Even a small overrun invalidates the declared premise. It need not cause a collision on that tick, but it removes the guarantee beneath every downstream stopping and lease-renewal margin. Response-time verification charges the full bound from equation 4.3, \(\LoadedEnforcerExecutionTime + \TaskBlockingTime_i + \TaskInterferenceTime_i \le \EnforcerDeadline\), in which \(\SharedHardwareInterferenceDelay\) inflates the execution term \(\LoadedEnforcerExecutionTime\), while \(\TaskBlockingTime_i\) and \(\TaskInterferenceTime_i\) remain the blocking and interference from the safety microcontroller’s own workload. Under the declared bound, shared platform hardware can introduce at most 60 μs of cross-workload delay before the enforcer violates its deadline, and every candidate in this chapter, chosen or rejected, is judged against that allowance.

Because a single permission tick at 1.3 m/s corresponds to only \(\SpeedAtDetection\,\PermissionLoopPeriod =\) 1.3 mm of vehicle displacement, engineers often underestimate the severity of a missed deadline. The stopping budget, however, leaves little margin for error. Under the baseline assumptions compiled in The warehouse mobile manipulator's stopping budget, bringing the vehicle to a controlled stop from 1.3 m/s requires 969.0 mm, which leaves 131.0 mm of clearance within the 1.10 m clear distance. That subtotal accounts only for the onboard stopping maneuver; it excludes the dispatch-to-admission latency \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\), which is an allocated system budget rather than a measured property. Deducting the 63.3 ms allocated to \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\) in notebook 13.3 shrinks the stopping reserve to 48.7 mm. While the stopping budget can absorb the loss of an isolated \(\PermissionLoopPeriod\), unmitigated microarchitectural contention does not produce predictable single-tick delays; it produces open-ended stalls that exhaust the stopping margin entirely. That asymmetry is why the thermal case of section 13.4 disqualifies candidate (b) even though each of its late refusals slips only about one tick. The distance lost is small, but the placement has broken the bound on which every distance was computed, and a broken bound no longer limits how far the next stall can run.

Guaranteeing that the enforcer finishes before \(\EnforcerDeadline\) also stabilizes the downstream feedback loop. When the system dispatches motor-drive command frames at a deterministic phase offset scheduled no earlier than \(\EnforcerDeadline\), execution jitter never propagates to the actuators. Instead, the feedback controller encounters a constant computation delay equal to 0.4 of the tick period. Control engineers model this fixed latency directly as phase lag at the loop crossover frequency, mirroring the phase delay introduced by a zero-order hold.

↪ The zero-order hold’s phase lag, about half a sample period of delay, is developed in Discrete-time sampling and zero-order hold phase lag.

13.7.2 Worst-case execution time analysis methods

Measuring ten thousand clean cycles on a quiet laboratory bench cannot reveal the worst-case stall that occurs when an out-of-order core suffers a cache-eviction cascade at the exact instant a DMA controller locks the memory bus. A declared worst-case execution time is a formal timing commitment, and systems engineers establish it through one of four methods, each resting on distinct premises (Puschner and Koza 1989; Wilhelm et al. 2008).

Static analysis operates entirely without executing the binary on hardware. The analyzer reconstructs the control-flow graph directly from machine instructions, bounds every loop iteration, and applies abstract interpretation (Cousot and Cousot 1977) to model the processor pipeline and cache states so that memory references are classified as definite hits, definite misses, or unresolvable states. The tool then solves an integer linear program (Li and Malik 1995) to identify the longest feasible execution path. It uses basic-block execution frequencies as decision variables constrained by structural flow conservation and loop bounds, while the objective function maximizes total cycle count based on block-level worst-case latencies. Static analysis yields a sound upper bound only with valid loop and path bounds and a model of the actual binary, memory and peripheral accesses, and relevant processor and resource states. The reference microcontroller’s in-order execution and private tightly coupled memory make these obligations more tractable, though they do not establish model fidelity by themselves. On application multicores, out-of-order execution, undocumented interconnect arbitration, and shared non-blocking caches make a static bound difficult to justify when relevant resource states and co-running demand remain uncontrolled or unmodeled.

Where an exhaustive microarchitectural model is unavailable, measurement-based analysis executes the binary directly on target hardware under the most demanding synthetic test harnesses the engineer can construct, appending an empirical safety margin to the observed maximum latency (the high-water mark). That directness explains its ubiquity in complex systems, yet it provides no mathematical guarantee against unobserved execution paths or rare microarchitectural collisions. The adversarial stress testing suite developed below targets contention states that ordinary workloads may leave unexercised, and its coverage limits remain part of the qualification record. Hybrid analysis bridges those two regimes. It measures the execution latency of isolated code snippets or basic blocks directly on physical silicon, then composes those empirical timings across the program’s control-flow graph using static path-analysis techniques. It thus relies on target measurements at the sub-routine scale while adopting the structural path bounds of static modeling. Alternatively, probabilistic analysis models execution times as random variables, fitting extreme value distributions to empirical latency traces to project the probability that an execution exceeds a given threshold (Cucu-Grosjean et al. 2012). Because this methodology assumes that experimental measurement runs faithfully sample the operational probability distribution, unmodeled dependencies on platforms with complex hidden microarchitectural state can invalidate the projection. Real-Time Timers, Watchdogs, and Schedulability analyzes these statistical models and their operational limits.

Cucu-Grosjean, Liliana, Luca Santinelli, Michael Houston, Code Lo, Tullio Vardanega, Leonidas Kosmidis, Jaume Abella, Enrico Mezzetti, Eduardo Quiñones, and Francisco J. Cazorla. 2012. “Measurement-Based Probabilistic Timing Analysis for Multi-Path Programs.” Euromicro Conference on Real-Time Systems (ECRTS), 91–101. https://doi.org/10.1109/ECRTS.2012.31.
Davis, Robert I., Sebastian Altmeyer, Leandro S. Indrusiak, Claire Maiza, Vincent Nelis, and Jan Reineke. 2018. “An Extensible Framework for Multicore Response Time Analysis.” Real-Time Systems 54 (3): 607–61. https://doi.org/10.1007/s11241-017-9285-4.

When migrating from an isolated processor to a shared multicore system-on-chip, isolated execution analysis becomes insufficient. Isolated worst-case latency must be augmented with explicit interference penalties for every contended shared resource. Modern multicore response-time frameworks construct these bounds by combining the formal service guarantees of shared arbiters with the maximum request demand generated by co-running cores (Davis et al. 2018). Such composition yields finite, provable bounds only when hardware mechanisms or operating system regulators enforce resource budgets, which is why section 13.6 relies on memory-bandwidth regulation (such as MemGuard) to render shared silicon analyzable. FAA AC 20-193 provides an acceptable means of compliance for airborne multicore systems. Where time interference is not mitigated, it calls for WCET analysis confirmed by testing on the target processor with all software executing in the intended final configuration. It also permits separate determination when time interference is shown to be mitigated (Federal Aviation Administration 2024).

On the running machine, the available execution models and enforced resource bounds determine the verification method. A discrete microcontroller reduces the shared-resource model, but analysis and testing must still cover local DMA, interrupts, internal buses, background admission, and the board-level supply, clock, and link faults inventoried here. On a shared application SoC, this example’s declared 250 μs remains the isolated baseline, not a loaded execution bound. To validate such a shared placement, the engineering team must formally bound \(\SharedHardwareInterferenceDelay\) across every shared architectural row identified in table 1. It must also verify that the resulting response bound remains safely within \(\EnforcerDeadline\), and it must execute an adversarial campaign capable of refuting both the analytical bounds and the isolation claims.

13.7.3 Adversarial stress testing and end-to-end brake onset timing

Refuting an independence claim requires synthetic workloads engineered to induce correlated worst-case microarchitectural contention rather than standard benchmarks (such as MLPerf) that report average throughput. Standard ML inference pipelines schedule memory transactions to maximize pipeline overlap and average data bus utilization, which conceals transient contention peaks. On physical silicon, maximum interference occurs during synchronized bursts when multiple subsystems demand the exact same physical resources at the exact same clock edge. An adversarial stress suite must synthesize these collision patterns deliberately. The five generators below target the shared die first, because that is where the strongest mechanisms of this chapter live, and they serve two campaigns: refuting a shared-die candidate, or qualifying an island an integrator can use.

  1. Targeted DRAM bank conflict generators: The stress suite issues unaligned DMA transfers with access strides calibrated to target the exact DRAM bank and bank group assigned to the enforcer’s memory buffers. By continuously alternating access across conflicting row addresses, the generator repeatedly evicts open row buffers, incurring the full precharge and activation latency (37.5 ns at the application SoC’s memory clock rate) on every subsequent read.
  2. Shared-cache miss-handling engines: Streaming memory threads cycle through footprints substantially larger than the shared last-level cache. These routines exhaust its MSHRs and saturate writeback buffers with dirty cache lines, triggering the structural interconnect stalls identified in section 13.3.2 as the primary threat to bounded enforcer latency.
  3. Resonant PDN current-step generators: Microbenchmarks execute rapid, high-amplitude current transients across neural accelerator arrays while sweeping the transient’s phase relative to the permission tick. High-bandwidth oscilloscope probes on the PDN capture package-level \(\PackageInductance \cdot d\AcceleratorSupplyCurrent/d\TimeInstant\) voltage droop and ringing, directly testing whether supply collapse induces clock throttling or logic faults on co-located cores.
  4. Correlated peripheral interrupt storms and storage saturation: High-bandwidth camera deserializers and NVMe storage controllers stream raw telemetry at line rate while asserting uncoalesced hardware interrupts. This flood stresses the interrupt controller, consumes shared crossbar bandwidth, and saturates system write buffers.
  5. Chassis electromechanical transient coupling: Test benches inject realistic traction-motor current transients and regenerative braking pulses into the shared DC bus. These tests verify whether DC-DC converter dropout, ground bounce, or electromagnetic interference couples into the enforcer’s power rails or clock distribution networks under worst-case operating envelope conditions.

The campaign on the chosen discrete microcontroller keeps only the generators that can reach it, and it maps onto the four fault-injection regimes of section 13.2, now applied together rather than one at a time. Supply transients on the shared battery bus (generator 5) are regime 1, power-rail droop testing. Clock margining under those transients is regime 2. Saturating the board link while the microcontroller’s own receive DMA, fieldbus interrupts, and background plan validation contend for its internal buses (section 13.6.2) is regime 3, link flooding. Streaming malformed and NaN-laden proposals during all of it is regime 4. The host-side targets of generators 1 to 4 do not reside on the microcontroller, which shares no DRAM, cache, or package with the proposer. Analogous contention from its own DMA and interrupts remains in regime 3. They drop out because (d) removes those rows, a claim the register readback and the inventory must support, not because the campaign overlooked them.

Hardware instrumentation must trace the complete perception-action timeline: sensor observation capture, coherent ingestion across the interconnect, permission decision completion, drive-frame dispatch over the real-time fieldbus, and physical frame arrival at the motor inverters. While digital bus logs reveal software delays, issuing a bus frame does not equal physical braking. For a controlled powered stop, synchronized motor-phase current probes and external optical tracking must measure the actual onset and dynamic trajectory of vehicle deceleration. The nominal renewal-to-onset budget is 82 ms, combining the software lease duration, one permission tick, fieldbus transit, and motor-drive torque development. Spring-applied mechanical brakes engage only when the vehicle has already decelerated to near-standstill, so mechanical brake contact cannot serve as a proxy for powered deceleration onset. In fault-injection scenarios involving total MCU failure or bus power loss, testing must independently verify the physical stopping distance delivered by the fail-safe emergency circuits. All timing intervals must be reported alongside verified clock synchronization bounds and instrument measurement uncertainties.

Because interference compounds across physical, thermal, and architectural domains, stress testing requires a full factorial experimental matrix to uncover paired-stress vulnerabilities. The evaluation sweeps across four orthogonal axes. The first axis is a cold condition (an illustrative junction temperature of \(35\,^{\circ}\text{C}\)) vs. a hot one, which on a shared die means junctions held at or above the throttling threshold \(\ThrottleTripTemperature\) of section 13.4 and on the discrete microcontroller means the bay at its highest qualified ambient. The second is an idle proposal path vs. a saturated proposal engine executing maximum-batch inference. The next is diagnostic circular logging disabled vs. streaming at full non-volatile memory bandwidth. The last is nominal tracking commands vs. boundary-violating commands that trigger refusal routines. Testing refusal paths under full stress is mandatory because executing a safe clamping trajectory or entering emergency shutdown involves cold branch paths, non-cached error-handling code, and secondary bus transactions that never execute during steady-state aisle driving.

The factorial matrix exists to uncover co-runner collisions that escape isolated unit testing. Consider what happens on the rejected shared-die placement when an unexpected obstacle steps into the warehouse aisle. Detecting the obstacle forces the enforcer to execute its cold refusal branch to initiate an emergency stop. Simultaneously, the application processor flags the anomalous visual scene and triggers a high-priority diagnostic core dump over the shared DMA interconnect. If tested in isolation, the refusal branch executes within its latency budget, and diagnostic dumping runs without error. When triggered concurrently, however, the diagnostic DMA burst floods the shared memory controller precisely when the enforcer must fetch its cold refusal instructions from DRAM. The resulting contention spike stalls the enforcer at the exact moment safe physical actuation is most urgent.

To see how empirical traces refute invalid isolation claims, evaluate an illustrative \(10^7\)-tick stress test conducted on the rejected shared-die configuration (where the enforcer executes on a real-time core cluster within the application SoC). Operating at one tick per 1 ms, this campaign runs for 10,000 s, or 2 h 46 min 40 s of continuous physical execution, excluding platform setup and reset overheads. Under cold, unloaded conditions, enforcer latency remains well within limits: the trace shows a median \(\text{p50} =\) 95 μs, a 99th percentile \(\text{p99} =\) 128 μs, and a maximum recorded latency of 152 μs. Under warm, heavily contended conditions, however, resource conflicts degrade the tail latency: \(\text{p50}\) rises to 210 μs, \(\text{p99}\) climbs to 330 μs, \(\text{p99.9}\) reaches 395 μs, and the observed maximum spikes to 468 μs.

Because the loaded \(\text{p99}\) alone exceeds the declared isolated bound \(\PermissionTickWcet =\) 250 μs, the trace invalidates the joint claim that the isolated baseline is bounded and proposal-side interference is zero. The overrun alone does not distinguish an underestimated baseline from interference; attributing it to interference requires independently justifying the baseline. Once the 90 μs of local blocking and RTOS task interference is charged, the loaded 99th percentile also pushes the response beyond the enforcer deadline \(\EnforcerDeadline =\) 400 μs. A loaded trace of this shape would reach the verdict that the thermal analysis of section 13.4 reached from a model, without relying on that model, and the verdict would stand even if the model were wrong. The percentiles here are illustrative; the point is that a real trace on the target, not an argument, is what can refute a placement.

Even if a 10-million-tick stress trace records zero deadline violations, that outcome demonstrates only that interference remained bounded across the specific microarchitectural states generated during the test. A qualification report must document the full latency distribution, observed maximums, total sample counts, and test durations, while explicitly listing all unexercised physical conditions, such as long-term power delivery degradation, transient electromagnetic interference, and silicon aging.14 When an empirical contention test fails, the engineering team must re-evaluate its architecture: partition memory channels, implement strict arbiter bandwidth caps, restrict proposal workload concurrency, or relocate the enforcer to physically isolated silicon. Conversely, when a campaign passes, it provides empirical evidence that supports the analytical model within the tested parameters, without mistaking a finite empirical sample for an absolute mathematical proof.

14 Transistor Aging (BTI and HCI): Bias temperature instability (BTI) and hot-carrier injection (HCI) gradually increase transistor threshold voltages over operational lifetimes. This shift degrades switching speeds across logic gates, an impairment exacerbated during transient voltage droops. Because engineers cannot measure multi-year degradation on fresh silicon, production designs incorporate explicit end-of-life timing guardbands. A physical stress campaign on new development boards cannot certify silicon against aging; the placement qualification record must formally log aging as an analytically budgeted, rather than empirically measured, condition.

Checkpoint 13.3: Refuting a placement

A placement claim is only as strong as the test that could have refuted it. Verify the core empirical criteria that defend this boundary:

13.8 The Placement Record

Selecting the discrete safety microcontroller on paper does not clear the mobile manipulator for release while its loaded execution fields remain unpopulated on the target hardware. The machine now has an assigned placement for its permission path, an explicit deadline, an unloaded execution estimate, and a factorial stress campaign designed to falsify hardware isolation, but none of those timing properties can support a release sign-off until they are recorded alongside the exact environmental and operational conditions under which they hold.

The placement record, the eleventh record in the record chain, formalizes where the safety permission path executes and supplies the physical evidence that its timing bounds remain invariant. It consumes the enforcement record of The Enforcement Record, inheriting the declared worst-case execution time and hard deadline of the permission path. To these timing constraints it binds the evidence that would show the bound holding under peak proposal load on production silicon. That evidence consists of the assigned execution domain, privilege ring, nominal loop period, unloaded and loaded observed maxima, AXI interconnect QoS arbitration settings, PDN inductive droop limits, thermal trip thresholds, enabled hardware mitigations, and a cryptographic digest of the external test report (table 4). An observed loaded maximum that exceeds the declared worst-case execution time immediately refutes the placement claim. Conversely, observed maxima that fall below the bound validate only the specific conditions exercised during testing; for any empirical latency distribution, sample maxima provide supporting evidence for an upper bound rather than an analytical substitute for it.

↩︎ That measured maxima support but cannot replace an analytical worst-case bound is established in Real-Time Command Transport and Bus Determinism.

The placement record bridges architectural configuration registers with direct physical measurements. Register readbacks of execution_domain_id and privilege_and_ring document the compute domain topology and execution privilege against architectural status registers, while shared_axi_channel_qos records interconnect arbitration configurations and verifies whether unprivileged software can reconfigure bus priorities before stress tests validate the resulting worst-case latency bounds. Alongside those digital settings, max_pdn_droop_thermal_trip ties voltage supply margins and junction temperature limits to high-bandwidth oscilloscope probe traces and on-die thermal telemetry gathered under full compute load. Together, enforcement_record_digest and test_report_digest establish cryptographic provenance by binding the placement record directly to its upstream timing contract and empirical test report.

The record architecture separates concerns into two distinct structures. A compact, fixed-size header stores critical platform configurations and observed execution maxima for low-overhead verification at boot time. This header anchors an external test report by its cryptographic hash. The report holds all non-fixed evidentiary records: complete hardware inventories, empirical latency distributions, total sample counts, environmental conditions, active hardware mitigations, and unresolved operational boundaries.

↪ Byte layout: The placement record’s field offsets and sizes are given in Placement record.

For the mobile manipulator, the header records the nominal 1 ms tick period and 400 μs hard deadline, while the test report archives the preliminary 135 μs unloaded execution estimate. Both observed-maximum fields remain initialized to all ones (0xFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFF), and the dedicated power rail cannot yet be documented as an approved mitigation because full empirical qualification remains pending. The loaded execution field remains unpopulated because no stress campaign has yet been executed on this machine. Populating this value requires running the factorial contention campaign of section 13.7 directly on physical hardware. In that campaign, the discrete microcontroller executes its safety loop while the host application processor saturates neural accelerator clusters with trajectory proposals and writes continuous trace logs at maximum junction temperature, triggering the refusal branch across stress trials. Every cycle time recorded during this contention benchmark must remain at or below the declared worst-case execution time, \(\PermissionTickWcet =\) 250 μs. In addition, the onset of powered deceleration, timed from the arrival of the last valid trajectory renewal, must occur within 82 ms. Until physical instrumentation confirms these bounds, the permission-independence claims of the safety case rest on unverified assertions, preventing any deployment safety release.

The external test report inventories all shared silicon and platform resources: memory channels, cache partitions, DMA engines, and power rails, alongside every competing bus master. For each resource, the report documents the hardware arbitration configuration and the resulting contention latency. For the safety permission rail, it records the selected 2 s hold-up capacitance and its pending qualification by a supply-loss fault-injection campaign. The report also defines the electrical isolation boundary by enumerating allowable rail claimants, restricting access strictly to the microcontroller, the gate-drive logic, and the spring-applied brake release coils, while entirely excluding unverified proposal-side compute loads.

Any contention measurement is meaningful only in the context of its exact operating state. The test report therefore qualifies each latency distribution with cryptographic hashes of the active neural model and system firmware, DVFS policies, enclosure thermal interface materials, ambient and junction temperatures, power rail voltages, interconnect arbitration weights, synthetic traffic generation profiles, sensor sampling rates, measurement instrumentation calibration, sample counts, and total test duration. The report records these parameters alongside full latency histograms and explicit enumerations of unexercised operational states, while the fixed header exposes only the scalar maxima for fast verification. For example, the \(10^7\)-tick trace evaluated in section 13.7 spans only 2 h 46 min 40 s of operation on a single physical machine. Such exposure demonstrates that the system survived specific benchmark conditions, but it does not exclude rare multi-contender collisions or provide a statistical bound across an entire production fleet.

Every placement record entry requires a predeclared falsification criterion formulated before executing hardware tests. This criterion defines the physical boundary that constitutes architectural failure, such as any safety enforcement cycle exceeding the declared 250 μs worst case during concurrent neural accelerator bursts and DMA transfers. The record documents the empirical execution distribution against this criterion and registers whether the isolation hypothesis survived or failed. When contention violates the latency budget and forces an architectural revision, the record details the specific mitigation deployed, such as configuring bandwidth throttling on the neural accelerator memory bus. The test entry must also explicitly report boundary conditions left unexercised during the test run, such as operating temperatures above the 45 \(^{\circ}\text{C}\) bay ambient, multi-bit error injection in tightly coupled memory, or cascading sensor transceiver failures. Adversarial Verification then consumes the documented arbitration points and shared power rails as a target map for hardware and software fault injection. It deliberately corrupts shared structures to test whether fault containment matches predicted isolation domains. Deployment Release incorporates the verified execution bounds and unexercised operational limits into the formal safety case, turning raw physical measurements into auditable evidence for real-world deployment.

With the placement record in place, the stopping budget is specified row by row for a wheeled mobile manipulator whose resident fallback is a brake-to-stop suffix on stable ground. A budget developed for one embodiment earns trust as a method only if it survives a machine with different dynamics. A legged humanoid, whose fallback trajectory must dynamically sustain balance while traversing unstructured terrain, provides the most demanding stress test of these principles (perspective 13.1).

Systems Perspective 13.1: When the fallback is a step

Question: Which rows of the stopping budget survive when the fallback is a step rather than a stop?

When hardware placement supports the deterministic execution contribution to the aggregate pre-brake delay \(\PrebrakeDelay\), the stopping-budget framework applies directly to the mobile manipulator’s powered braking maneuver, with dispatch-to-admission delay allocated and drive-energy qualification still open. However, a bipedal humanoid walking mid-stride possesses no stationary equilibrium into which it can instantaneously brake. Its fallback maneuver is dynamic, a recovery step. Every row of the stopping budget must consequently be re-evaluated for dynamic stepping:

  • Observation age survives. Temporal latency continues to translate directly into spatial displacement, the conversion stated in Bounding Observation Age. On an articulated sensor head undergoing angular rotation, this error scales with viewing distance: a feature at range \(\FeatureViewingDistance\) traverses space at tangential speed \(\TangentialSpeed = \AngularSpeed \FeatureViewingDistance\) under an angular rate \(\AngularSpeed\). For instance, an uncalibrated 10 ms camera–IMU temporal offset during a 1.5 rad/s head pan misidentifies the position of an obstacle located 5 m ahead by 7.5 cm.
  • Commitment semantics change. The latched zero-velocity trajectory suffix established in Behavior at the Seam is replaced by a certified recovery stepping trajectory. Rather than terminating in a stationary rest configuration, this contingency plan must be admitted alongside the nominal motion command to guarantee safe placement of the swing foot onto an admissible support polygon.
  • The safety inset changes. The geometric obstacle clearance margins derived in Safe Sets as Conditional Permission no longer suffice to guarantee dynamic balance. A recovery step demands full closed-loop hybrid dynamics, relative-degree constraint analysis, identification of reachable capture regions, and actuation limits under torque saturation. Kinematic tracking uncertainty and ground reaction force disturbances must propagate directly into this forward invariance set before a valid safety inset can be calculated.
  • The placement record expands. Because a zero-joint-velocity command mid-stride does not establish a balanced terminal state, the hardware isolation boundary must encompass all computational, interconnect, and power resources required to execute the active recovery step. Just as the wheeled manipulator’s placement record isolates the energy storage and gate drivers needed for powered braking, the biped’s placement record must certify dedicated bus bandwidth, joint controller execution enclaves, and surviving drive-energy paths for active balance recovery. Rail hold-up preserves supervisory logic; it does not by itself supply the recovery step.

13.9 Fallacies and Pitfalls

Placement determines the physical resources shared by complex proposal algorithms and critical enforcement routines. Traces of normal operation cannot prove these systems are truly independent when rare physical events, such as power voltage transients, clock faults, memory contention, or a stalled producer, stress those shared hardware pathways.

Fallacy: Logical privilege rings protect real-time enforcers from shared-rail electrical droop.

Privilege checks govern digital access, while the rail supplies the gates that perform those checks. The illustrative droop calculation predicts a 39 percent gate-delay increase under its stated model; only device characterization can determine the resulting setup margin or reset behavior. Lockstep cores sharing a rail can share a failure, so comparator coverage does not establish supply independence. The selected isolated rail removes one coupling path and still requires common-supply fault tests.

Pitfall: Sharing phase-locked loops or reset controllers across proposal and permission domains.

A separate core can still stop when a host-controlled reset asserts or slow when a shared governor changes its clock. The topology must trace each clock and reset to the controlling hardware, then test the relevant faults under load. Naming a controller a safety island does not establish that these dependencies are independent.

Fallacy: Low average memory bus utilization guarantees bounded read latency for real-time safety tasks.

Average utilization leaves the arrival order and longest queue unspecified. Plain FIFO queueing at the SoC’s full bandwidth is not the danger, as notebook 13.1 showed. The read tail comes from an FR-FCFS controller that keeps passing the safety read in favor of a streaming accelerator’s row hits, or from the miss-handling contention of section 13.3.2, and neither shows in an average. In an illustrative counterexample, such a tail stretches the snapshot read to 320 μs, which leaves 80 μs before the 400 μs deadline, less than the 95 μs needed for evaluation and staging even before blocking and interference are charged. A service bound, qualified bypass, or independent local memory addresses that failure; a low average does not.

Pitfall: Using blocking software mutexes across inter-processor communication boundaries with untrusted neural policies.

A mutex held by a stalled proposer transfers control of the enforcement deadline to that proposer. An unbounded seqlock retry loop has the same defect when publication remains odd. The selected boundary protocol makes one read attempt per tick and discards a collision. That discard leaves an active valid command in force only while its own evidence, state, and lease remain admissible; it neither resets the lease nor forces premature revocation. Memory ordering and bounded copy time remain part of the placement evidence.

13.10 Summary

Part I built the mobile manipulator on a hypothesis: its learned proposer runs on application processors and accelerators and its permission path on a bare-metal microcontroller. This chapter turned that hypothesis into a placement decision that evidence can refute. The allocation alone does not separate the two paths, because privilege boundaries govern none of the physical resources the two paths still share. Partitioning therefore requires a justified timing bound and fault evidence, with loaded tests able to refute the claim.

The evidence must cover the physical resources that the placement still shares. A loaded test supports a scoped claim about its workload, arbitration policy, supply, clock governance, and thermal conditions. Separate processor packages remove some coupling paths, but the board’s common supply and communication links remain part of the argument. Across the link, the boundary contract keeps a stalled proposer from holding the permission deadline. The reader makes one bounded attempt per tick and discards a torn record, and a windowed watchdog trips on feeds that arrive too early as well as too late.

The placement record carries that evidence forward. It takes the permission path’s declared worst-case execution time and deadline from the enforcement record and adds the loaded observed maxima for the declared test conditions, the shared-resource queue and power headroom, the common power, clock, reset, and memory failure paths, and the conditions left untested. The machine’s own record holds a 135 μs illustrative unloaded estimate against the declared 250 μs worst case inside the 400 μs deadline. It still awaits loaded test results, which must name the arbitration policy, clocks, sample count, and physical endpoint before any release claim can rest on it.

The observation contract, belief record, intent lease, trajectory record, enforcement record, and placement record now describe one machine. Its timing must hold across the chain, including the memory, power, and communication paths between stages. Placement makes the execution premise of principle 3 falsifiable through a threshold, a stress campaign, and a record of what remains untested. Part IV inherits the conditional stopping margin, the unverified premise that aisle crossings are procedurally restricted (Sizing the composite stopping budget), and the placement record’s empty loaded field. It also inherits a dispatch-to-admission interval \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\) that this chapter has allocated at 63.3 ms, inside the 100.8 ms the spare can pay for, but not yet measured. Seven relations carry the chapter’s quantitative argument, and table 5 records each with the decision it settles and the place it is derived.

Key Takeaways: Silicon placement and memory contention
  • Logical separation is a hypothesis about hardware: Privilege rings govern which addresses a task may touch, not where its requests wait, which rail feeds it, or which clock and reset tree it depends on. Loaded tests can refute the claim; justified bounds and fault evidence must support it.
  • Averages cannot bound a deadline, and bandwidth is not the main threat: On a shared die at full bandwidth, more than 82 FIFO backlogs must precede the snapshot read before it crosses the declared worst-case execution time. Shared-cache miss handling is far stronger: measured slowdowns of 21× and more than 300× cross the declared bound once 4.3 percent or 0.28 percent of the tick is miss stall.
